## **Introduction** 

Rogerio Oliveira rogerio.oliveira@morganstanley.com 

Welcome to the first edition of the Emerging Markets Quantitative Quarterly (EMQQ). The main objective of this new publication is to offer a compendium of the quantitative work and models developed by Morgan Stanley’s Emerging Markets and AXJ Strategy Groups in the previous quarter. 

The quantitative work has been organized within EMQQ into three main sections related to the pertinent asset class: credit, rates and currencies. Work on derivatives will generally fall within the correspondent asset class. We have also added a fourth category named “cross assets/global/other assets” to encompass the pieces that fall within more than one category and/or that have global scope. 

The first example of a global piece is a primer on Morgan Stanley Quotient Analytics (MSQ), our web-based analytics platform that has recently been materially upgraded to provide comprehensive coverage of emerging markets assets including currencies, rates, sovereign and corporate credit. 

Every edition of the EMQQ will have an introduction discussing any possible updates related to pieces referenced in previous versions of EMQQ and the descriptions and summaries of the main EM quantitative work recently developed by the strategy teams. After the introduction, these main pieces are re-printed here without any changes or updates. 

We aim for the EMQQ publication to enjoy long shelf life and be a one-stop reference for investors to obtain details about the models, methodology and number-crunching processes behind the generation of the trade ideas. Hence, clients should be aware that the numerical results and trade ideas provided in each piece of this compendium may be outdated by the time EMQQ goes to print.  For most models the updated results are being systematically displayed elsewhere, and we provide the links to these periodical publications and reports. 

In summary, EMQQ´s main objectives do not include focusing on the results obtained from the models at the time of the publication but, rather, discussing the actual models and methodologies, the assumptions and numerical procedures, their strengths and weakness, possible constructive criticisms, improvements and alternative approaches. 

EMQQ will serve as a first step, we hope, towards integrating and leveraging the best quantitative work being developed across Morgan Stanley.  General suggestions are more than welcome and, in particular, discussions and comparisons with other quantitative work displayed elsewhere with potential for cross-fertilization of ideas and positive externalities are greatly encouraged. 

With the formalities of the introduction behind us, we now turn to the description of the quantitative work recently developed by our strategy team. We hope you enjoy reading this publication as much as we enjoyed compiling it. 

## **Credit** 

## **Debt Sustainability** 

In this first edition of EMQQ we have two pieces on sovereign credit. These pieces do have some overlap but are largely complementary tools to analyze opportunities in the sovereign debt asset class. 

The first piece is _An Exercise in Debt Sustainability_ , July 19 2010, by our CEEMEA strategist Chuan Lim and our CEEMEA economist Pasquale Diana. This model is based on a traditional debt sustainability framework where the future path of debt to GDP ratio is simulated under various assumptions. 

The key macro-economic variables used as inputs by the model are initial debt to GDP ratio; average interest rates paid on the debt, convergence of real effective exchange rate, 

short-term and steady state growth forecasts and expected fiscal effort in the form of primary surplus. Most of these inputs are provided by our EM economics research team. The equations in the appendix of their piece provide the mechanical dynamics of the debt to GDP ratio:

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

**September 2010 Emerging Markets – Quantitative Quarterly** 

rate. Total debt/GDP (dT) is split into external debt (dE) and local debt (dL). 

This model is very useful as a first cut view on the relative creditworthiness of different countries. The general idea is that countries with worse or deteriorating trajectories for the debt to GDP ratio have higher probability of achieving unsustainable debt levels and eventually being forced into debt restructuring. The credit spread curves of these countries should command a higher premium to account for this risk. 

The debt sustainability model benefits from its simplicity, objectivity and relative robustness. The inputs are purely based on fundamentals and, hence, outputs are usually quite stable and only change gradually, although sizeable shocks such as the ones observed in H2 2008 may substantially alter the variables and the conclusions of the analysis. 

The model was implemented for 15 important EM countries. However, since the key inputs are readily available for most countries worldwide, this analysis is easily extendable to peripheral EM sovereigns and can also shed light on debt sustainability concerns currently pending over some developed nations. Chuan and Pasquale use European Commission data to conclude in their piece that CEE countries seem to be in relatively good shape compared to Greece, Italy, Spain and Portugal.

Source: Morgan Stanley Research 

However, the relative value view of creditworthiness that results from the model is probably better suited for the comparison of external debt credit spreads and CDS spread levels across different countries. The usefulness of debt sustainability models for the cross-country analysis of local rates is less clear, in our opinion. Nonetheless, Chuan and 

Pasquale also make successful use of their analysis to infer interesting results over the relative performance of 5y5y forwards of the local curves in Poland and Hungary. We elaborate more on this later in the _Rates_ section of this introduction. 

Even if the probabilities of default are assumed to be the same for local currency debt and for external debt, local credit spreads (usually unobservable) are, in theory, only a fraction of regular external credit spreads because of the quanto effect from the positive correlation between FX depreciation and credit spreads widening. The modeling of this quanto effect is something we plan to address in future work and might be useful for structured products such as local currency CDS. 

In any case, the local debt defaults are often less likely than external debt restructures. This is probably because the decision to default on the local currency debt is mostly a political one as countries can often partially monetize their local debt and experiment with the trade-offs of the risks of hyperinflation and costs associated with debt restructuring. 

Hence, we believe the drivers of CDS spreads and local rates are different and require different models. Please see the description of our Brazil local rates risk premia model in the _Rates_ section. 

Possible enhancements of the model include adding uncertainty. The equations used to govern the dynamics of the debt to GDP clearly assume deterministic evolution. In practice the model is computing the average or expected path of the debt to GDP ratio. 

However, looking only at the average of a stochastic process may be misleading. For example, a random walk has an unaltered expected future path. However, because of its evergrowing volatility, it endures a high probability of reaching any unsustainable level, no matter how high it is. Clearly, a debt to GDP ratio that has a sustainable expected path but high probability of becoming unsustainable is not very reassuring for investors. The volatility of the debt to GDP ratio is, hence, an important variable and can be obtained as a simple combination of the volatilities and correlations of growth, primary surplus, etc. 

Adding volatilities and correlations to the simulation of debt to GDP paths certainly has the cost of reducing simplicity, but that is probably more than compensated for by the enrichment of the analysis. Countries with more stable macroeconomic policies, solid track records on the fiscal front and countercyclical primary surplus should in general enjoy extra benefits

**September 2010 Emerging Markets – Quantitative Quarterly** 

in the simulation and attain reduced probability of sustainability concerns. 

Although similar analysis conducted in this piece is probably already partially reflected in the credit rating of each country, other variables and factors may often be taken into account by the rating agencies. In general we find it very reassuring to have an in-house model to be able to stress assumptions and compare results under different inputs and scenarios. 

## **Sovereign Ranking** 

The second credit piece in this first edition of EMQQ is _SCRM and SovRank,_ July 22 2010, by Regis Chatellier. The SovRank model aims to cross-analyze and rank the attractiveness of sovereign CDS spreads of different EM countries. SovRank includes three different models: the fundamentally driven Sovereign Credit Rating Model (SCRM), the technical model Credit Curve Mean Reversion (CC-MR) and the Credit Volatility Adjusted (C-Vol) model. 

The _SCRM_ is an econometric model that regresses the composite rating (average rating of Fitch, Moody’s and Standard & Poor’s) of several countries (70 countries in the sample) over sets of macroeconomic explanatory variables such as GDP growth, fiscal balance, inflation, ranking of governability, dummy for geostrategic influence, etc. 

Given the forecasts from the Morgan Stanley Economics’ _Global Forecast Snapshots_ report, the linear regression model predicts the average rating of each country for the forthcoming year. Actual composite forecasts may be compared against the model outputs to obtain indication of upgrade or downgrade pressures for each country. 

To minimize errors, hedge against inertia from the rating agencies and significantly reduce the potential deviations of the model, two different specifications using two different sets of explanatory variables are provided for _SCRM_ . The results are combined by taking the simple average of the output of each regression. In the future, we may introduce further specifications of the _SCRM_ and scenario analysis framework to gauge the impact on EM country creditworthiness under various market and macroeconomic conditions. 

Use of _SCRM_ started earlier this year, and it correctly indicated several of the EM rating upgrades and DM rating downgrades that the rating agencies made in 2010. 

The next step is to fit a simple exponential regression (excluding the less liquid names) between composite rating and the level of 5-year CDS to define the “fitted” credit curve. Some DM countries are included to anchor the regression for the [AA-AAA] rating bucket, and countries with rating B+ or below are excluded because the distribution of single B credits is far more dispersed and compromises the stability and the reliability of the model. 

Thereafter, the cheapness (or the richness) of a credit versus predicted fundamentals is calculated as the difference between current CDS and ‘fair value’, i.e., at which level the credit should be trading over time based on the predicted rating suggested by the _SCRM_ model on one side and its corresponding CDS on the credit curve on the other side. 

The second building block of SovRank, the Credit Curve Mean-Reversion ( _CC-MR_ ) model, is meant to statistically capture the technical market dynamics of the credit curve and its short-term deviations from fair value. 

The same credit spreads versus ratings exponential curve that had been fitted in the _SCRM_ model is used to define the “fair value”. Market credit spreads are assumed to meanrevert and have normal distribution around their average spreads to “fair values”. The ‘cheapness’ or the ‘richness’ in basis points terms are then calculated as the difference between the current CDS level and its historical average spread relative to the “fitted” credit curve. 

The third and final component of SovRank is the Credit Volatility-Adjusted ( _C-VOL_ ) Model, where CDS levels are normalized by dividing by their volatilities and multiplying by the average volatility of the market. The resulting volatilityadjusted CDS is then used in the exponential regression versus composite rating.

SovRank combines the three models (SCRM, CC-MR and C- VOL) by averaging the cheapness/richness in basis points for all countries according to each model and dividing it by the CDS level of each country. The standard deviation of this measure of relative attractiveness is taken across the whole sample and countries are labeled ‘ _Cheap-_ ‘ / ‘ _Rich-_ ‘ or ‘ _Cheap+_ ‘ / ‘ _Rich+_ ‘  if their deviation is smaller than or larger than 1 standard deviation. 

We find the SovRank model an extremely useful tool for relative value analysis and it should easily extend to sovereign bonds and even corporates that have quality historical data. 

Among its shortcomings, SovRank implicitly assumes credit ratings are correct on average across countries at each point in time. Because of the inertia of the rating agencies, this may 

be compromised after a large global shock such as the one experienced in H2 2008. Extending the _SCRM_ to include scenario analysis, as planned, may mitigate this pitfall somewhat. Also, enhancing the Macro econometric model from the current simple cross-country regression to include time series/panel data would give it flexibility to allow for general market over- and undervaluation from time to time. 

Finally, complementing SovRank with some sort of debt sustainability analysis as discussed earlier in this introduction would probably contribute significantly to a better final outcome. 

The results from the SovRank model are being periodically updated in _Credit Rich & Cheap Watch_ , Sept 08 2010, by Regis Chatellier and Robert Tancsa.

## **Rates** 

## **Tightening Cycles and Curve Flattening** 

This rates section starts with the discussion of three ratesrelated research reports where there is a strong intersect in the analysis developed as well as the results achieved: 

- _De-Composing the Behaviour of Asian Rates Curves_ by Pieter Van Der Schaft and Rohit Arora, July 30 2010. 

- _Hiking Cycles and Flattening Trends in CEEMEA_ by Chuan Lim, July 14 2010 

- _Hiking Cycles and Term Spreads in Latin America_ by Rogerio Oliveira and Juha Seppala, May 26 2010. 

In all of these pieces the curve dynamics are studied under previous hiking cycles. For most of the countries in all the three regions, the results from these independent analyses are similar: with different lags across countries, local rates curves tend to flatten as central banks embark upon tightening modes. 

Some of our recent trade recommendations have been substantiated and partially based on the findings of these reports. (Please see _Chile: Enter 1s5s Camara Flattener_ , June 04 2010 and _Peru: Buy 5Y Soberano,_ June 16 2010, by Juha Seppala and Rogerio Oliveira). 

The analysis was conducted separately for each individual country, and the number of historical hiking cycles per country and even per region was too small for rigorous time series econometric analysis. Hence, the main result is that curve flatteners offer good risk-rewards around the start of the hiking cycles, but this is based only on anecdotal evidence. 

However, we believe this makes intuitive sense as a large part of the risk premium is compensation for the uncertainty about the future direction of monetary policy. While the market is uncertain about the timing of the start of the hiking cycle, a non-trivial portion of the long-term rates is due to risk premia, part of which tends to evaporate as consensus is formed about the beginning of the hiking cycle, pushing the curve to a flattening bias. 

Furthermore, similar results across the three regions are not only reassuring but also increase the number of historical observations to the point where a panel data regression might be statistically meaningful. This cross-country/region analysis, which we hope to address in future work, should confirm the stylized fact uncovered in these previous reports and perhaps point to other important drivers of curve dynamics.

Beyond the analysis of tightening cycles and flattening trends, the Asian rates piece relies on principal component analysis to offer a very rich analysis of the drivers of some specific Asian local curves. We will not elaborate on that analysis here but encourage clients who are interested in the Asian rates markets to read Pieter and Rohit’s report and explore all of its idiosyncrasies. 

## **Risk Premia Model (Brazil Rates)** 

The last rates piece, _How to Value Brazil Rates?_ , July 29 2010, by Rogerio Oliveira and Juha Seppala, presents an econometric macro-finance model whose objective is to carve out a fair measure of risk premia given some observed and forecasted variables. 

The choice of Brazil as the first country for this analysis was based on the best combination of relevance of local markets and availability of detailed historical consensus data about the expected path of the overnight rates (called Selic in Brazil). 

To implement the model, we first compute the historical empirical risk premia for various tenors of the DI curve as the difference between the market curve and the curve implied by compounding the expected future path of Selic interest rates. 

Thereafter, we run time series regressions of these empirical risk premia over macro and financial explanatory variables. Some variables reflect global financial conditions (VIX, EM

CDX, etc.), while others account for idiosyncratic financial aspects of the country (FX volatility and sovereign CDS spreads) – and we also add a few to capture macroeconomic conditions (activity or production indices, price indices, etc.).

Source: Morgan Stanley Research 

Finally, we use our regressions and observed and forecast inputs to compute fair risk premia and their dynamic evolution. The fair risk premia estimated are then combined with Selic rates expectations to price all the DI contracts. 

To substantiate the results of the model, we conduct historical back-testing by comparing ex-ante rate change predictions of the models with ex-post realizations. According to this metric, the success rate of the model is around 70%. 

This was our first frontal assault on the complex problem of estimating the fair risk premia of local interest rate curves. We plan to gradually extend and adapt this model to several other local rates curves. However, this is far from trivial and will need to be addressed on a country by country basis. 

One of the obstacles is the lack of historical consensus data for expected overnight rates in many countries. However, this drawback may be partially overcome by developing an enhanced Taylor rule for each country. 

Other challenges are related to the focus on important country/region specific variables and circumstances. For example, in CEEMEA expectations about convergence to the EUR zone tend to pin down long-term rates, and in Asia the general historical low level of rates and high correlation with US rate movements demand modifications to our current framework. 

Going back to the discussion in the _Credit_ section of this introduction, Chuan and Pasquale managed to make successful use of their debt sustainability model to infer interesting results over the relative performance of 5y5y forwards of the local curves in Poland and Hungary, despite our bias that local rates analysis should probably be conducted with different models. 

The expectations of convergence to the Eurozone, which seems to be an important factor governing the dynamics of the long end of some local curves in CEEMEA, is probably what increased the importance of debt sustainability analysis in this particular case. This convergence feature has not yet been properly addressed in our local rates risk premia model. As we move to expand that model beyond Brazil's DI curve and into other EM local markets, we take positive note of Chuan and Pasquale's successful 5y5y local rates analysis as possible guidance. 

The results of the Brazil’s fair risk premia model are currently not being systematically updated in any recurring publication and, so far, only appear in trade ideas or strategy update pieces where the computations of fair value are required. However, as we expand the set of countries covered by the model, we plan to make the results available soon, at least on a weekly basis.

## **Currencies** 

## **Fair EMFX Implied Volatility** 

The first work on currencies included on this first edition of EMQQ is _How to Value EMFX Implied Volatility?,_ June 28 2010, by Rogerio Oliveira and Juha Seppala. 

This report discusses an econometric model that uses financial variables (VIX, CDS spreads and historical volatilities) and macroeconomic variables (trade balance, CPI, reserve level, etc.) to evaluate and forecast fair implied volatilities across several Emerging Market currencies. The model may be seen as a macro-finance enhancement of a dynamic ARCH type model. 

The results from the regression were back-tested by elaborating buy/sell signals for a simple and transparent deltahedged trading strategy where the simulated P&Ls are computed using the actual changes in implied volatilities and time decay of the options. The strategy is designed to take advantage of the mean-reverting feature in implied volatility, which is obviously salient from the negative coefficient of vol(t) in Exhibit 5.

A buy (sell) signal is triggered whenever the spread between the forecast implied volatility and the current market implied volatility is above (below) one (minus one) standard deviations (using 90-day rolling computations). The trade is closed when this spread crosses zero or changes signs. 

The Sharpe and Sortino ratios calculated from the simple trading rule are quite reasonable, and this encourages us to use the results of the model as a first indication of the richness and cheapness of the market implied volatility. More complex trading strategies may achieve even better riskrewards. 

The regressions are run on a country by country basis, and different variables receive different weights in different countries. For example, in Mexico the most important variables were current volatility and FX reserves, whereas in Russia VIX and IP are crucial. A possible extension of this model would be to leverage the cross-sectional analysis by running a panel data regression and highlighting the most important variable across all countries. 

The results of this methodology are currently being updated in the weekly publication _FX Options – Trade Ideas and Data Pack_ , Sept 14 2010, by Ronald Leven, Corentin Rordorf and Juha Seppala. 

## **Hedging FX Inconvertibility Risk** 

The last of piece of this compendium elaborates on the (tail) risks and hedging strategies of an inconvertibility event of BRL, one of the most important EM currencies  that is still only semi-convertible. 

The new currency laws approved by Venezuela earlier this year brought FX control risks back into the spotlight. Most investors can only repatriate money at the parallel ‘market’ exchange rate, which is currently only about half as valuable in USD as the official exchange rate. 

In contrast, in Brazil the local legislation seems to be moving towards making BRL eventually convertible. Nonetheless, 

**9** 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

**September 2010 Emerging Markets – Quantitative Quarterly** 

there is always a residual risk about the quality of policies of future administrations, and investors with local exposure should consider protecting their portfolios against the tail risk of an inconvertibility event. 

In a nutshell, a long BRL inconvertibility protection strategy may be the composition of a long position in USD/BRL through offshore FX forwards (NDF) and a short exposure in USD/BRL through onshore forwards, which are not directly available but they may be synthetically constructed using short-dated FX futures, BRL rates futures (DI curve) and local USD rates futures (CUPOM cambial curve). 

The implementation of the hedge can be materially simplified by using inconvertibility swaps instead of multiple forwards and futures, and when using tenors beyond 2y, this might be the only viable alternative to clients. Furthermore, the inconvertibility swap is a more effective hedge than buy/sell offshore/onshore forwards when investors want to protect a fixed USD notional. 

Exhibit 7 displays the different payouts (in case there is a triggering event) between acquiring inconvertibility protection through the two different hedging strategies (onshore/offshore forwards pair or inconvertibility swap). The first protection is applied to a BRL amount equal to the USD Notional x USD/BRL FX Strike, not to the USD amount. Since currency devaluation tends to follow an inconvertibility event, part of the protection is likely to be compromised. On the other hand, the inconvertibility protection from the swap is guaranteed to be over the full USD notional amount, and therefore is more effective in a crisis or in a stress scenario, in our view. 

## Exhibit 7 

Inconvertibility and credit risks are highly correlated and some investors might be tempted to hedge their portfolios using CDS due to better liquidity. 

However, inconvertibility swaps, although illiquid, might be better suited for corporate, private equity and other investors with money invested onshore because inconvertibility events often precede and even occur in absence of sovereign defaults. For example, Venezuela imposed convertibility restrictions in 2003 but has not defaulted on its debt up to this date. 

Furthermore, the cost of a BRL inconvertibility swap is often somewhat cheaper than buying CDS protection. We have even observed periods when bullish exuberance towards Brazil has pushed the inconvertibility spread into negative territory.

## An Exercise in Debt Sustainability 

Exhibit 1 

**CE-3: Debt-sustainability maths tell an uncomfortable truth for Poland.** Our simulations, based on mechanic assumptions, reveal that lack of fiscal reform will push the Polish debt/GDP ratio to much higher levels. Our results represent simulations based on a traditional debtsustainability framework, and we acknowledge that a similar exercise done five years ago would have shown very different results. Even so, this exercise shows that Hungary has, as things stand, the _best_ fiscal dynamics in terms of debt/GDP among the CE3, assuming unchanged policies. Of course, however, the key assumption behind our base case probably overemphasises risks in Poland and the Czech Republic and underestimates risks in Hungary. 

**Extending the debt-sustainability analysis to other EM countries, we find that most countries in Asia and LatAm should continue to reduce their debt/GDP levels, albeit at variable speeds.** In Asia, we expect the debt/GDP reduction to be driven by high real GDP growth, despite the countries running primary deficits.  Even though we expect the real interest cost burdens to be higher in the LatAm countries than in Asia, debt/GDP reduction is still possible under our simulations, thanks to steady-state real GDP growth of between 3.5% and 5.5%, coupled with primary surpluses of between 2% and 3% of GDP (except Mexico). 

**By regressing CDS and ASW against debt/GDP levels, the countries that stand out as cheap on a relative value basis (on both external and local debt) are Turkey, Colombia and South Africa, while Malaysia and Brazil appear expensive.** As fundamentals continue to improve in Turkey and Colombia, we think it makes sense to favour Turkish and Colombian bonds. 

Another observation is that Peru local bonds appear cheap, while Peru CDS spreads are too low. **Therefore, one interesting trade is to buy the Peru local bonds, and go long Peru CDS.** Indeed, our LatAm strategists are currently recommending long Peru Soberano bonds (see _Peru: Buy 5y Soberano_ , Rogerio Oliveira and Juha Seppala, June 16, 2010).

## **An Exercise in Debt Sustainability** 

## **CE3: Poland Does Not Shine as Bright** 

In view of the focus on the fiscal situation in DM, particularly in GIPS (Greece, Italy, Portugal, Spain), we have conducted an exercise on medium-term debt simulation in Central Europe. 

**We want to emphasise upfront that these are mechanical simulations that should not in any way be interpreted as actual forecasts.** Rather, they present plausible paths for the debt/GDP ratio under different scenarios. Our simulations are based on a traditional debt-sustainability framework. The ultimate path of government debt/GDP will depend on future nominal GDP growth, interest rate and fiscal outcomes. Note that a similar exercise done five years ago may have shown very different results. 

## **We build three scenarios, based on subjective** 

**assumptions.** In our _base_ case, we assume unchanged policy in CE3, using the European Commission debt and primary balance forecasts for 2010 and 2011 as initial guidance. From 2012 onwards, we plug in our assumptions of ‘steady-state’ real GDP growth, inflation, interest rates and primary balance.  For the primary balance, we take the EC’s measure of the CAPB (cyclically adjusted primary balance) for 2012 as a gauge of how the _structural_ primary balance looks. 

In order to smooth the profile, we also assume a three-year ‘transition’ period from the 2011 primary balance to the cyclically-adjusted primary balance. The intuition is that we leave policy as it currently is and we give the cycle three years in order to return the primary balance to its steady state. Having done that, we see what happens to debt/GDP. Please see the Appendix section for details on the methodology and assumptions. 

**Under our baseline ‘no policy change’ simulations, Hungary’s debt/GDP is expected to fall from 81% currently, and stabilise at just under 80% of GDP.  Of concern, debt/GDP for the Czech Republic and Poland are forecast to rise over the next five years and beyond, mainly because both countries are assumed to run a primary deficit over the analysis horizon, by virtue of the assumption stated above.** 

In order to maintain the government debt/GDP on a steady path beyond 2013 (see Exhibits 12 and 13), Poland needs to see its steady-state real GDP growth at 7.1% (model assumption: 3.5%) or steady-state primary deficit at 0.2% of GDP (model assumption: 3% deficit).  For the Czech Republic, the steady-state real GDP growth needs to be 

raised to 9.2% (assumption: 2.8%), or the primary account needs to be balanced (assumption: -3.3%).  These are by no means simple tasks, in our view. 

We would highlight that, given our methodology, our base case is likely overly pessimistic on the Czech Republic and Poland (it assumes that policy does not get tightened) and overly optimistic on Hungary (our base case essentially assumes that the current tight fiscal stance holds _ad infinitum_ ). Of course, as stated above, assuming that fiscal policy is kept stable from 2011 onwards (‘tight’ or ‘loose’ at the 2011 level) may be a stretch, but this exercise is mechanic in nature.

## **Scenario Analysis on CE3** 

**Our base case is probably too mechanistic.** Therefore, in this section, we tweak our primary balance assumptions, and compare how our optimistic and pessimistic scenarios for CE3 compare with the base case (see Appendix for assumptions). 

We think it makes most sense to proceed on a country-bycountry basis, rather than applying the same assumptions across all three countries. The reasoning is that Hungary has already tightened its belt a great deal over recent years, and now boasts one of the _strongest_ structural (i.e., cyclically adjusted) positions in the whole of the EU (see Exhibit 3). **So, our ‘optimistic’ scenario for Hungary broadly assumes that it maintains this discipline over the coming years.** 

**For the Czech Republic and Poland, which have been far less proactive of the fiscal front, the optimistic scenario is that they actually take measures to correct the structural deficit.** For all three countries, we use EU

**Czech Republic:** As an optimistic scenario, we assume that the primary deficit improves by 0.5% of GDP every year, starting from 2012 (-3.1% of GDP versus -3.6% in 2011). This should bring the country to a primary surplus of 0.9% of GDP in 2020.  In this case, debt/GDP increases from 41% in 2010 to around 55% in 2017, stabilising thereafter. 

As our _base_ case already assumes unchanged fiscal policy following the recent deterioration, we think that our _pessimistic_ scenario should not deviate too far from the base case.  For the pessimistic scenario, we assume that the primary deficit deteriorates by 0.1% of GDP per year, starting from 2012. Our model shows debt/GDP rising to 80% by 2020.

**Poland:** The assumptions used are similar to those for the Czech Republic.  As an optimistic scenario, we assume that the primary deficit improves by 0.5% of GDP per year, starting from 2012 (-3.5% of GDP versus -4.0% in 2011).  This should bring the country to a slightly positive primary balance in 2020 (which Poland last enjoyed in 2007, at the peak of the boom). In this case, debt/GDP increases from 54% in 2010 to around 68% in 2017, stabilising thereafter. 

As our base case already assumes unchanged fiscal policy, we think that our pessimistic scenario should not deviate too far from the base case.  For the pessimistic scenario, we assume that the primary deficit deteriorates by 0.1% of GDP per year, starting from 2012.   Our model shows debt/GDP rising to above 90% by 2020. 

Note that we have only assumed fiscal tightening in Poland in the optimistic scenario.  Even in the optimistic scenario, debt/GDP is projected to breach the 60% threshold. This result underscores the mechanic nature of the analysis: Poland’s constitution forbids it from running _any_ deficit once debt/GDP hits 60% of GDP (see Exhibit 8). Nevertheless, one conclusion still stands: barring a change in the constitution or a redefinition of debt (say, to exclude pension liabilities of the second pillar[2] ), the Polish adjustment will have to be really quite severe over the next few years. 

> 2Note that in terms of pension reform, the CEE countries (Poland and Hungary in particular) have made far more progress than their Western European counterparts. Hungary and Poland are ranked among the countries where the impact of ageing on the public finances is among the smallest in the EU (see _Public Finances in EMU – 2010_ , p 68). The Czech Republic remains a laggard in CEE on the pension reform side.

## **Overview of Polish Fiscal Rules** 

**Public debt between 50% and 55% of GDP in year t** The draft budget for year t+2 must not propose a higher deficit-to-revenue ratio than in year t+1. This applies to every territorial entity (i.e., to local governments also) 

**Public debt between 55% and 60% of GDP in year t** Draft budget must not increase the ratio of State Treasury (practically the same as central government) debt to GDP in year t+2. Also, no increase in public sector salaries, no new loans from the State Budget and no revaluation of pensions above CPI (in year t) is allowed. Finally, the cabinet must present an adjustment programme to parliament to take the debt/GDP ratio down 

**Public debt above 60% of GDP in year t** Any government borrowing is forbidden in year t+2, and ministers have one month to provide an adjustment programme to take the debt ratio below 60% 

Source: Morgan Stanley Research 

## **Polish 5y5y versus EUR Too High?** 

**As fiscal deficit and debt/GDP levels make up two of the four Maastricht criteria, we think that convergence probability of Hungary and Poland should be reflected in the 5y5y spread to EUR, to a certain extent.** A high fiscal deficit and debt/GDP level tends to result in increased issuance, which pushes the long-end yields higher, thus implying a lower chance of convergence, and vice versa. 

Since March 2009, the HUF 5y5y spread to EUR (currently around 270bp) has recovered from the high of 400+bp, bottoming twice at around 180bp, which is just 40bp shy of its five-year low of 140bp.  On the contrary, the PLN 5y5y spread to EUR did not recover like HUF 5y5y did, despite the rally in the risky markets.  In fact, it widened further over the past year, and is now at 180bp, close to the all-time high of around 210bp (low was at around 20bp in 4Q08).

**July 19, 2010 EM Profile** 

**This divergence in 5y5y trend reflects the different routes the Polish and Hungarian governments took in 2007/08.** The Hungarian government had to tighten its fiscal policies in order to comply with the IMF’s restrictions.  On the other hand, the Polish government loosened its fiscal policy over recent years in order to stimulate growth, and managed to steer the economy away from a recession, outperforming its CE3 neighbours. Relaxing fiscal policy at the peak of the cycle was a risky strategy on Poland’s part (see also _Complacency Breeds Trouble_ , September 27, 2007), and it ~~—$$$____~~ meant that the fiscal position was much weaker than the headline numbers suggested. 

Based on our analysis, while the government debt/GDP is higher in Hungary, the pace of increase should be much slower than Poland’s. If one assumes that policies remain broadly stable from here, Poland’s government debt/GDP is projected to catch up with Hungary’s around 2020 if our base case materialises. Of course, that assumption has many drawbacks, as discussed above, and significant tightening kicks in as the debt ratio approaches 60%. 

In addition, Komorowski’s win in the Polish presidential election should be viewed as a positive for the fiscal reform, in our view, though probably it is of limited impact over the next few months. **This could put a ceiling on the PLN 5y5y spread to EUR, although we cannot discount the spread reaching new highs if the fiscal situation remains unresolved for an extended period of time.** 

## **Comparisons with GIPS** 

**Compared to GIPS, the situation in CE3 seems relatively contained.** According to the European Commission, by 2015, Greece could see its government debt/GDP rise from 137% currently to over 160%, if we see an unsuccessful consolidation[3] . 

Government debt/GDP levels in Spain (64% to around 90%) and Portugal (84% to around 110%) should also experience a steep upward trajectory due to their large primary deficits. Although already at 118% now, Italy’s government debt/GDP profile is the least concerning out of the four, where the EC projects the debt/GDP to stabilise at around 120%. 

If these countries follow a successful fiscal consolidation programme, the EC expects the government debt/GDP levels to fall or stabilise, except in Portugal, which should still see a rise in its government debt/GDP level. 

> 3 For consistency purposes, we compare the debt/GDP for GIPS under the ‘Unsuccessful Consolidation’ scenario as our model makes a similar assumption post 2013 for the base case.

## **Debt-Sustainability Analysis on EM Countries** 

**Extending the debt-sustainability analysis to other EM countries, we find that most countries in Asia and LatAm should continue to reduce their government debt/GDP levels, albeit at variable speeds.** In Asia (India, Indonesia and Malaysia), the government debt/GDP reduction is expected to be driven by high real GDP growth, despite running a primary deficit of between 1.3% and 1.8% of GDP. Our Asian economists are forecasting 5-8% long-run, steadystate growth beyond 2013 in these countries. 

**Even though the real interest cost burdens are expected to be higher in the LatAm countries than in Asia, government debt/GDP reduction is still possible, thanks to steady-state real GDP growth of between 3.5% and 5.5%, coupled with primary surpluses of 2-3% of GDP.** The only exception is Mexico, where government debt/GDP is expected to stay fairly constant at around 34% (mainly due to our expectation of a small primary deficit). 

Real GDP growth in the CEEMEA region is expected to be the weakest, with steady-state real GDP growth at 4.5% or below. CE3 and Russia are expected to underperform the rest of the countries in the region. **The three countries in CEEMEA that are assumed to run a primary deficit over the analysis horizon are the Czech Republic, Poland and Russia.** 

Russia should see its debt/GDP doubling from 9.5% to 18% over the next five years, but we would emphasise that this move is from a low base, as Russia currently has the lowest government debt/GDP among all the EM countries, and the debt-service costs should be relatively easy to fund.

|Israel|1.8|-0.3|-2.1|
|Peru|2.5|0.2|-2.3|



Source: Morgan Stanley Research estimates 

From the table, we can see that the **CE3 countries need to increase real GDP growth by 0.3-6.4% to stabilise the debt/GDP profile, or increase the primary surplus by 0.53.3% of GDP.** For Russia, in order to stabilise debt/GDP at around 15% beyond 2013, the long-run real GDP growth needs to be at close to 14% per year (model assumption 3%), or the primary surplus needs to be at 0.2% of GDP (compared to our steady-state forecast of -1.5% of GDP). 

Mexico needs to see its real GDP growth at 3.8% (versus 3.5% assumed) or running a primary deficit of 0.2% of GDP, in order to maintain a steady debt/GDP profile beyond 2013. With a relatively low debt/GDP level (28%), Indonesia’s situation is one of the least severe among the countries with a rising government debt/GDP profile.  However, it can still stabilise the debt/GDP profile by increasing real GDP growth by 1.5% to 7.8%, or moving its primary deficit from 1.8% of GDP to 1.3%. 

## **Putting it All Together** 

In Exhibit 14, we plot the current level of 5y CDS against debt/GDP in 2011. **On a relative value basis, Indonesia, South Africa, Turkey and Colombia appear cheap versus Peru, Malaysia and Brazil, while Russia, Mexico, India and Hungary appear fairly valued.** The results seem consistent when we plot 5y CDS against debt/GDP in 2013 (where the steady-state assumptions start). 

We have excluded Argentina and Israel from the plots as their current CDS levels distort the charts significantly. 

Argentina’s 5y CDS is very high at around 900bp, and should be treated separately from the other countries in this analysis. At 80% of debt/GDP, Israel’s 5y CDS is around 200bp lower than Hungary’s. Several factors that contribute to the difference are: 1) A better fiscal and growth outlook for Israel; 

**16** 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

## **July 19, 2010 EM Profile** 

2) Israel is A rated while Hungary is BBB rated; 3) The absence of balance sheet problems/FX loans in Israel’s banking sector; and 4) The availability of the US-guaranteed loans to Israel.

Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg 

Aggregating both the comparison results, **the countries that stand out as cheap on a relative value basis are Turkey, Colombia and South Africa, while Malaysia and Brazil appear expensive.** As fundamentals continue to improve in Turkey and Colombia, we think that it makes sense to favour Turkish and Colombian government bonds.  However, we would caution against selling/going underweight Malaysian government bonds as the fundamental improvements could justify the premium embedded in these bonds. 

Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg 

Next, we compare the local debt valuation versus debt/GDP. The most relevant measure is the asset swap spread (ASW[4] ) on the 5y debt (note that the regression fit for ASW is not as strong as the fit for CDS versus government debt/GDP). 

**Turkey, Colombia, Peru and South Africa appear cheap versus Russia, Indonesia, Mexico, Malaysia and Brazil.** Similarly, the results seem consistent when we plot 5y ASW against debt/GDP in 2013. 

Another observation is that Peru local bonds appear cheap, while Peru CDS spreads are too low. **Therefore, one interesting trade is to buy the Peru local bonds, and long Peru CDS.** Indeed, our LatAm strategists are currently recommending long Peru Soberano bonds (see _Peru: Buy 5y Soberano_ , Rogerio Oliveira and Juha Seppala, June 16, 2010). 

> 4 Theoretically, the lower the rating of a country (e.g., due to higher debt/GDP level), the cheaper their local bonds should trade versus the equivalents in swaps (i.e., the ASW should be wider).  However, we would highlight that there are other factors that can affect ASW as well, e.g., liquidity.

## **Analyst Stock Ratings** 

Overweight (O or Over) - The stock's total return is expected to exceed the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months. Equal-weight (E or Equal) - The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months. Not-Rated (NR) - Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. Underweight (U or Under) - The stock's total return is expected to be below the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months. 

## **Analyst Industry Views** 

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index.

## **EM Profile** 

## SCRM and SovRank 

**We update our Sovereign Credit Ratings Model (** _**SCRM**_ **) using Morgan Stanley Economics’ updated macroeconomic forecasts and highlight potential future rating actions for EM countries.** 

**We introduce the** _**SovRank**_ **model, which utilises** _**SCRM**_ **results and gauges the attractiveness of EM sovereign credit default swaps (CDS) contracts.** 

**We detail the three components of the model – fundamentals, technicals and market risks.** 

We provide an update of the _SCRM_ model[1] using Morgan Stanley Economics’ latest macroeconomic forecasts. _SCRM_ is aimed at predicting the potential rating upgrades and downgrades for EM sovereigns. _SCRM_ highlights potential upgrades for EM overall, but most notably for Turkey, Israel, Qatar and Indonesia. The model is also flagging some potential downgrades, including Venezuela, Mexico and Poland. For reference, in the DM space, Spain and Italy are the countries with the highest risk of further downgrade according to the model. 

_SovRank_ has been developed to provide an objective framework to assess the attractiveness of EM sovereign CDS. The model combines three main components, namely fundamentals, technicals and market risks, each of these components being assessed quantitatively using specific models: _SCRM_ , _CC-MR_ and _C-VOL_ , respectively. Eventually, the model scales the attractiveness of each credit depending on its degree of ‘cheapness’ or ‘richness’. 

As we run _SovRank_ on current CDS levels (combining the _SCRM_ , _CC-MR_ and _C-VOL_ ), we highlight the attractiveness of Hungary, Romania, Bulgaria and Israel. By contrast, Panama, Mexico and Indonesia appear relatively expensive based on the same criteria.

## **SCRM and SovRank** 

In this report, we provide an update of our Sovereign Credit Ratings Model ( _SCRM_ ), utilizing the latest macroeconomic forecasts from Morgan Stanley Economics. 

We also introduce our _SovRank_ methodology, which is aimed at providing an assessment of the relative richness and cheapness of EM sovereign CDS contracts. The model is in fact a combination of three models ( _SCRM_ , _CC-MR_ and _CVOL_ ), each of them assessing one specific key driver of the market: fundamentals, technicals and market risks. We explain the methodology of each model in the sections that follow, and how _SovRank_ combines these three components. 

This section highlights the key results of both the _SCRM_ and _SovRank_ . The remainder of the report details key aspects of the various _SCRM_ and _SovRank_ model methodologies. 

_SCRM_ highlights potential upgrades for EM overall – to BBB-, and most notably for Turkey, Israel, Qatar and Indonesia[2] . The model is also flagging some potential downgrades, including Venezuela, Mexico and Poland. 

We detail more aspects of _SCRM_ and its recent track record in the _SCRM_ methodology section that follows.

**July 22, 2010 EM Profile: SCRM and SovRank** 

## **Sovereign Credit Rating Model (** _**SCRM**_ **)** 

Our Sovereign Credit Rating Model ( _SCRM_ ) aims at predicting the future level of sovereign credit ratings based on key macro inputs. As both ratings and CDS levels are intrinsically linked to the credit quality of a country, _SCRM_ also has direct market valuation implications[3] . 

We will update the _SCRM_ model results every quarter, following the release of Morgan Stanley Economics’ _Global Forecast Snapshots_ report. The results will depend on the evolution of the model inputs, which are likely to change quarter-to-quarter. The _SCRM_ results, therefore, are intended to be dynamic as our macroeconomic forecasts change. 

_SCRM_ is an econometric model which assumes that key macro variables determine country creditworthiness and by extension the level of credit ratings, and that the rating agencies themselves adjust their assessment of country ratings with a certain lag with respect to these variables. 

We provide two model specifications, _SCRM1_ and _SCRM2_ , in order to maximise the efficiency of the model.[ 4] _SCRM_ model results represent the simple average of _SCRM1_ and _SCRM2_ , unless otherwise noted. 

We intend to introduce further specifications of _SCRM_ in the near future, to further enhance the scope and power of the model. We will also introduce a scenario analysis framework to gauge the impact on EM country creditworthiness of varying market and macroeconomic conditions. We also plan to introduce a corporate credit version of the model. 

The _SCRM1_ specification includes the following variables: 

- _1) GDP per capita (log)_ 

- _2) General government debt/GDP_ 

- _3) Fiscal balance/GDP_ 

- _4) Inflation (%YoY)_ 

- _5) Dummy for sovereign default in the past 10 years_ 

- _6) Dummy for sovereign default since 1980_ 

- _7) Ranking of governability (based on the World Justice Project Rule of Law index)_ 

- 8) _Dummy of geostrategic influence_ 

By regressing these variables versus the composite average rating between Fitch, Moody’s and Standard & Poor’s, we obtain a linear model predicting the average rating for the forthcoming year. The distribution of the errors of _SCRM1_ – calculated as the average number of notches away from the actual composite rating – is shown in Exhibit 3. 

## Exhibit 3 

## **Actual versus Predicted Ratings (** _**SCRM1**_ **)** 

**==> picture [183 x 137] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
1.00<br>0.80<br>0.60<br>0.40<br>0.20<br>0.00<br>-0.20<br>-0.40<br>-0.60<br>-0.80<br>-1.00<br>Actual versus Predicted (notches)<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


Source: Morgan Stanley 

At this point, it is important to stress that _SCRM1_ is not intended to be a gauge of rating agency actions _per se_ . It is rather intended to estimate how key credit metrics impact a measure of overall sovereign creditworthiness, which is manifested in the credit rating. Moreover, we find that rating agency assessments have considerable inertia, and this can have a bearing on specific rating assessments not captured in _SCRM1_ . 

In order to minimise the errors of _SCRM_ in predicting the rating actions, we model another specification, _SCRM2_ , which includes the following variables: 

- _1) A lag of the dependent variable (the average composite sovereign credit rating)_ 

- _2) Real GDP growth_ 

- _3) Fiscal balance/GDP_ 

- _4) Current account balance/GDP_ 

- _5) Inflation (%YoY)_ 

- _6) Ranking of governability_ 

- _7) Dummy of geostrategic influence_ 

> 3 _SCRM_ is a useful starting point for comparative analysis, but for a complete understanding of sovereign risk, additional analysis must be undertaken on a case-by-case basis. 

> 4The calibration of the model was performed based on 2004-08 data for 70 countries. However, the fit of the model was back-tested in and out of sample over a period of 10 years. See Exhibit 3 and Exhibit 4. 

The distribution of errors of _SCRM2_ versus observed ratings is given in Exhibit 4.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

## **July 22, 2010 EM Profile: SCRM and SovRank** 

## Exhibit 4 

## **Actual versus Predicted Ratings (** _**SCRM2**_ **)** 

**==> picture [180 x 147] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
1.0<br>0.8<br>0.6<br>0.4<br>0.2<br>0.0<br>-0.2<br>-0.4<br>-0.6<br>-0.8<br>-1.0<br>ss F FF CF FS<br>| Actual versus Predicted (notches)<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


Source: Morgan Stanley 

Eventually, we combine _SCRM1_ and _SCRM2_ to predict the changes in ratings. In the present case, we take the simple average of the results given by the two models, this process reducing very significantly the potential deviations of the _SCRM_ model. 

_SCRM_ is a useful starting point for relative value analysis as it gives a good indication of potential upgrades and downgrades. In order to estimate to which extent the market is pricing such upgrades and downgrades, we map CDS levels against prevailing credit ratings and draw an exponential regression to define a synthetic credit curve[5] . 

Although the correlation between credit ratings and CDS is imperfect, it is still very significant: as we use an exponential regression (excluding the less liquid names) to define the credit curve, the average correlation (R²) between composite rating and the level of 5-year CDS is 74%[6] . In the short term, the discrepancy between credit ratings and what the market is implicitly pricing in terms of risk premium can diverge, but in the long term, the two variables are even more correlated. 

To the extent that ratings and CDS are indeed correlated in the long term, the _SCRM_ model can therefore be used to determine long-term CDS levels based on projected ratings (i.e., projected fundamentals). From this relationship, we can also determine what the market-implied credit rating is for each sovereign name and compare it to the _SCRM_ results.

As we try to estimate the cheapness (or the richness) of a credit versus predicted fundamentals, we calculate the gap between current CDS and ‘fair value’, i.e., at which level the credit should be trading over time based on the predicted rating suggested by the _SCRM_ model on one side and its corresponding CDS on the credit curve on the other side. Eventually, this difference in basis points measures the potential shift of the CDS to reflect the change in rating, as predicted by the _SCRM_ model. 

## **Recent Assessment of the** _**SCRM**_ **Model** 

In January 2010, we presented a first overview of the _SCRM_ model based on economic estimates for 2009 and forecasts for 2010. 

At the time, we highlighted the discrepancy between emerging markets and developed markets, the model pointing to substantial upgrades for several EM countries and downgrades for a series of DM countries (see _Strategy Update – EM versus DM: Value versus Perception_ , January 5, 2010). 

From the countries covered by the model in January, _SCRM_ accurately predicted the upgrades for the following: 

- Chile: June 16 to Aa3, Moody’s 

- Qatar: July 5 to AA, S&P 

- Lebanon: March 31 to B, Fitch and April 13 to B1, Moody’s 

- Turkey: January 8 to Ba2, Moody’s and February 19 to BB, S&P 

> 532 countries are part of the exponential regression: Brazil, Bulgaria, Chile, China, Colombia, Czech Republic, Egypt, Hungary, India, Indonesia, Israel, Kazakhstan, Korea, Malaysia, Mexico, Panama, Peru, Philippines, Poland, Qatar, Romania, Russia, South Africa, Thailand, Turkey, Australia, Austria, France, Germany, Japan, Sweden and the United Kingdom. 

- Indonesia: January 25 to BB+, Fitch and March 12 to BB, S&P 

- South Korea: April 14 to A1, Moody’s. 

> 6Calculated over a period of one year. 

**28** 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

**July 22, 2010 EM Profile: SCRM and SovRank** 

Note that, year to date, no EM country has been downgraded, which highlights the overall upgrade trend for EM[7] . 

As a matter of fact, _SCRM_ had correct predictions, and although the full extent of the upgrades was not always fully captured (especially for Lebanon), the model gave the correct directionality of the rating actions. 

One exception should be noted though: Argentina. Back in January, the model was indeed pointing towards a half-notch downgrade on average when the country was actually upgraded by Fitch to B from B- on July 12. Arguably, the model tends to be less accurate for those countries rated single B, given the greater variability of the results and the importance of willingness-to-pay factors (in particular the political factors, which are difficult to quantify). Excluding Argentina, and based on the nine cases of accurate predictions, we therefore conclude that _SCRM_ is a fairly reliable predictor of sovereign ratings actions. 

As we update our forecasts on EM economic data (see _Global_ ~~—_———~~ _Forecast Snapshots_ highlights further potential upgrades, in particular for Turkey , June 10, 2010), the _SCRM_ model (from BB to BBB-), Israel (from A to A+), Qatar (from AA to AA+) and Indonesia (from BB to BB+). The model also signals several potential downgrades in the EM space, including Venezuela (from B+ to B-), Mexico (from BBB to BBB-) and Poland (from A- to BBB+). 

The model may also be a useful starting point for comparative work and prompt further analysis. For example, _SCRM_ predicts a convergence in ratings for Hungary and Poland, at BBB+, and downward ratings pressure for China over the forecast horizon. These results may well represent limitations of the model itself, but may also stimulate further discussion on the sovereign credit outlooks. 

In the case of China, the country’s relatively low GDP per capita proves a constraint on the model’s determination of ratings. A more subjective assessment – or alternative specification than that ascribed by _SCRM1_ and _SCRM2_ – may well de-emphasise the importance of GDP per capita in the creditworthiness determination (and/or increase the emphasis of other factors) of China. Indeed, the recent experience of the sovereign credit turmoil in high-income DM countries is instructive in this regard; that said, _SCRM_ has thus far accurately projected the downward ratings trajectory (and magnitude in most cases) of DM sovereigns. 

> 7Note that other EM countries were upgraded over the period but were not covered by the model results from January 2010, including Panama, Dominican Republic, Estonia and Saudi Arabia.

We now discuss the components of the _SovRank_ model. _SovRank_ is one of several approaches we intend to use to gauge valuations in EM sovereign credit. The model is a combination of three models ( _SCRM_ , _CC-MR_ and _C-VOL_ ), each of them assessing one specific key driver of the market: fundamentals, technicals and market risks. We explain the methodology of each model in the sections that follow, and how _SovRank_ combines these three components. 

## **Credit Curve Mean-Reversion (CC-MR) Model** 

The credit curve, defined as the CDS level versus rating, is driven not only by fundamentals but also by market/technical factors. As opposed to the _SCRM_ model, which focuses on fundamentals, the Credit Curve Mean-Reversion model ( _CCMR_ ) is meant to capture statistically the market dynamic of the credit curve and how each country is trading versus fair value. 

considerations (supply/demand, risk appetite, etc.). To the extent that the model assumes no changes in fundamentals in the short term, the CDS of each country is meant to revert to the level it usually trades versus the credit curve once technical factors also mean-revert. 

For our purpose, we use the same exponential regression as in the _SCRM_ model to define the credit (fitted) curve, the regression serving as the reference point to gauge the meanreversion dynamic.[ 9] As it is typically the case for meanreversion models, the differences between CDS levels and the average spread to ‘fair value’ (fitted curve) are assumed to follow a normal distribution. This assumption allows calculating Z-score indicators[10] , probabilities of meanreversion, and detecting ‘rich’ and ‘cheap’ countries versus the credit curve based on Z-scores. 

As per our back-testing analysis, the mean-reversion towards ‘fair value’ is a reasonable assumption of the _CC-MR_ model: as we compute an average Z-score per region, we note the tendency of CDS to revert to their average level versus the credit curve, the fair value being synthetically defined by an exponential regression (see Exhibits 9-11). 

Eventually, we gauge the ‘cheapness’ or the ‘richness’ in basis points terms as the difference between the current CDS level and its average level relative to the credit curve. 

At this point, it is important to reiterate that we do not consider the regression of CDS versus rating as the fair value _per se_ , but it serves as a reference point for a certain category of credit risk. In fact, the model does not consider the absolute value of this spread to regression as relevant, but rather its deviation from the average level. 

Note we include DM countries in the regression for the sample not only to be more representative of the whole credit space, but also to anchor the regression to the [AA-AAA] rating bucket, the latter being less volatile by definition in terms of risk. 

The _CC-MR_ model is assuming that rating upgrades and downgrades are slow to take place[8] and short-term movements in CDS levels are mainly due to market 

> 8As per our calculations based on 76 emerging countries and three rating agencies, rating changes (excluding changes in outlooks) occur every three years on average. 

> 9We deliberately exclude the distressed/high-beta credits such as Argentina, Hungary, Lebanon, Lithuania, Romania, Ukraine and Venezuela from the analysis. The model tends to be less reliable for these credits, given the weight of political (‘willingness’) factors. 

> 10Zs = (CDS - 1Y average)/1Y standard deviation.

## **Credit Volatility-Adjusted (** _**C-VOL**_ **) Model** 

Considering the CDS level versus rating is arguably not enough to assess the attractiveness of a country. Indeed, credit ratings are given as long-term assessments for investors, and for this reason they are not always adjusted according to the very latest economic developments. By contrast, markets tend to react immediately (and sometimes overreact) to these developments. 

CDS levels _per se_ are not enough either to gauge the market risk for a defined credit. In essence, CDS contracts are meant to gauge the risk of default but not necessarily the embedded market risks, which are much more volatile by nature. The main component missing is indeed volatility; it is only by combining the level of a CDS (the ‘reward’ for the seller of protection) and the volatility of the contract (the ‘risk’) that we can effectively gauge the attractiveness of a credit from a pure market perspective. 

In order to reflect market risks, we adjust the CDS of each country by its level of volatility. Concretely, we divide CDS levels by the volatility[11] and normalise the result multiplying by the average volatility of the market[12] . By doing so, we assess the relative level of each credit versus the regression line, the countries trading above being considered as ‘cheap’ (i.e., expensive price of protection against default when considering volatility/market risks), the countries below this line being considered as ‘rich’. 

As was the case with the _CC-MR_ model, we include DM countries and exclude both less liquid credits and countries which average rating is equal or lower than B+. Despite this correction, however, the quality of the regression for the _C-VOL_ model[13] is far less than in the case of the _SCRM_ and _CC-MR_ models (where CDS levels are not volatility-adjusted). In fact, we consider the regression line as a simple reference to spot which countries are trading above (cheap credits) and below (rich credits) the regression line rather than an actual fair value for CDS vol-adjusted levels. 

Although it is difficult to assess precisely the degree of cheapness or richness with this process, we still consider the concept as relevant. In fact, the _C-VOL_ model is particularly relevant when combined with other models, in our view.

As we correct CDS prices by the level of volatility, the attractiveness (‘rich’ or ‘cheap’) of a country can substantially differ from the static analysis of CDS levels versus rating. Compared to a static model, some countries appear much more expensive when adjusting by their volatility, in particular Kazakhstan, Russia and Lithuania, while others look quite attractive, including China, the Philippines and Brazil. 

## _**SovRank**_ **Model: Combining Fundamentals, Technicals and Market Risks for a Proper Assessment** 

In order to assess the attractiveness of a credit, our _SovRank_ model combines the three models mentioned above, i.e., _SCRM_ for fundamentals (macro), _CC-MR_ for technicals (statistical analysis) and _C-VOL_ for market risks (volatilityadjusted analysis). 

Practically, we calculate the cheapness/richness in basis points for all countries according to each model, and take a simple average[14] of the results in order to gauge the attractiveness of each credit. 

Eventually, the model ranks the ‘cheapness’ (or ‘richness’) of each credit. For this purpose, we define the relative attractiveness (Ai) of a country ‘i’ as the ratio between its cheapness in basis points[15] and the market level of the CDS for this particular country – intuitively, the ratio gives a sense of how cheap or rich this country is trading relative to its current level. 

11Defined in the present case as the standard deviation of the CDS relative to the credit curve over a one-year period. 

12We reflect the volatility of the market not by the average but via the median of the sample as the average volatility can be largely impacted by distressed credits. 

> 13The correlation coefficient (R²) is around 33%, compared to 74% for the _CC-MR_ model, mainly because the dispersion relative to the fitted curve is higher. 

> 14We may modify the simple average process in the future by a weighted average as we test in practice the efficiency of the model. 

> 15Average cheapness as given by the _SCRM_ , _CC-MR_ and _C-VOL_ models.

On the other side, we calculate the standard deviation (S) of all _Ai_ indicators as a basis of comparison among countries. Based on the above, we can rank the ‘cheapness’ or the ‘richness’ of each country as follows: 

- ‘ _Cheap+_ ’ status is given to a country _i_ whenever Ai>S 

As we run the _SovRank_ model (based on the three factors: fundamentals, technicals and market risk), we highlight the cheapness of several countries, including Bulgaria, Hungary, Romania and China (the latter due to very low volatility in comparison to the market). By contrast, Mexico and Panama appear relatively expensive using the same assumptions (see 

Exhibit 13). 

- ‘ _Cheap-_ ‘ status is given to a country _i_ whenever 0<Ai<S 

- ‘ _Rich+_ ’ status is given to a country _i_ whenever Ai<-S 

- ‘ _Rich-_ ‘ status is given to a country _i_ whenever -S<Ai<0

## **Key Themes** 

**Bull Flattening / Bear Steepening coupled with a high degree of volatility** : Since the outbreak of the financial crisis in mid-2008, USD and Asia ex-Japan (AXJ) yield curves have started to behave very differently. Whereas the US curve has traditionally been characterized by bull steepening and bear flattening moves in line with Fed policy cycles, since January 2009 the US curve has instead seen bull flattening and bear steepening moves – curve trends last seen between 1994-2000 when the Fed was on hold for a prolonged period of time. 

## **Asian curves show more divergent behaviour** 

Compared to the US, Asian rates markets show more divergent curve behaviours.  In terms of trends in curve slopes, Asian rates curves can be roughly classified according to the following three regimes: a) Domestic Policy Cycle, b) US Trend Followers, and c) US/Domestic Curve Trends. 

## **Key Takeaways** 

We have analyzed the divergent curve behaviours in Asian curves and summarize our key takeaways from the diverging behaviour as follows: 

- Asian Curve Regimes have been fundamentally different from the US curve since early 2009. 

- In the absence of Central Bank policy guidance the long-end volatilies suggest uncertain growth outlooks. 

- Lower long-end yields will lead to lower volatility. 

- Low and front-end vols imply low rate move expectations. 

- Inability of PCs to completely explain the curve behaviours explain the new regime. 

- Curvatures could remain depressed as investors earn the carry. 

- The Outliers: contrary to the global curvatures, THB and TWD bellies are relatively cheap.

## **De-Composing the Behaviour of Asian Rates Curves** 

## **Key Takeaways** 

We analyze the behaviour of Asian rates curves. Our key takeaways are as follows. 

- Asian curve regimes have been fundamentally different since early 2009, while correlations with USD rates have declined sharply – primarily in 5-10yrs. 

- In the absence of central bank policy guidance and given macro-economic uncertainties, rates and curve volatilities have risen.  However, lower long-end yields will eventually lead to lower realised and implied vols. 

- Low and range-bound front-end vols confirm market expectations for gradual monetary tightening. 

- PCA fails to fully explain the new regime and meanreversion trades of past trends may not work. 

- Curvatures could remain depressed as investors focus on earning carry at lower absolute yields. 

- Although this has led to a richening of 3-7yr USD, HKD IRS rates, THB and TWD bellies look relatively cheap

## **US Rates: Bull Flattening/Bear Steepening Coupled With a High Degree of Rates Volatility** 

Since the outbreak of the financial crisis in mid-2008, USD and Asia ex-Japan (AXJ) yield curves have started to behave very differently. Whereas the US curve has traditionally been characterized by bull steepening and bear flattening moves in line with Fed policy cycles, since January 2009 the US curve has instead seen bull flattening and bear steepening moves – curve trends last seen between 1994-2000 when the Fed was on hold for a prolonged period of time.  (Exhibit 1) 

However, in comparison to the 1994-2000 period, realized volatility of 10yr US Treasuries and of 2-10yr UST and USD IRS curve slopes has been historically high over the past 2 years (see Exhibit 2). We believe this reflects: 

- a) a high degree of market uncertainty concerning the US and global economic outlook (moderate growth, double-dip recession or debt-deflation?); 

- b) investor risk aversion, which has not only spurred safehaven flows into US Treasuries, but may have also led to a higher risk premium being attached to duration risk; 

- c) a reduction in capital and VAR trading limits among banks to trade and absorb record levels of US Treasury issuance. 

## Exhibit 1

## Exhibit 2 

## **The Realized Vol of USD IRS 2s10s curve (3m) Has Remained Elevated Over the Past Two Years** 

Moreover, as Exhibit 3 show, the USD, HKD and SGD curves have been more stable and predictable and less volatile than the AUD, KRW and MYR curves, which have been characterized by frequent changes in curve slopes/ direction coupled with a high degree of curve volatility (notably in AUD). 

## **USD-Asia Curve Correlations Have Declined Over the Past 12 Months, but More so for 5-10yr Curve Slopes Despite Policy Tightening** 

Note: The realized volatility is the annualized rolling 63 day standard deviation of the daily differences in the curve slope. Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg 

## **Asian Curves Show More Divergent Behaviour** 

Compared with the US, Asian rates markets show more divergent curve behaviours.  In terms of trends in curve slopes, Asian rates curves can be roughly classified according to the following three curve regimes: 

- **Domestic policy cycle** : RBA, RBNZ, BoK and RBI policy cycles are the main drivers of AUD, NZD, INR and KRW curve slopes, which have seen typical bear flattening/bull steepening trends during periods of policy tightening/ easing; 

- **US Trend followers** : the HKD and TWD curves have most closely followed trends in the US curve over the past 10 years, i.e. bull steepening and bear flattening from 2000-2008, followed by bear steepening and bull flattening during 2009-2010.  The SGD curve too has generally tracked the USD curve closely, except in 20092010 when bull flattening was followed by bull steepening; 

- **US/domestic curve trends:** the MYR and THB curves generally follow US curve trends.  However, at times these curves diverge from the US curve as a result of diverging policy cycles (e.g. surprise policy tightening in Malaysia), shifts in the size and composition of government issuance and/or incidental corporate liability hedging flows. 

## Exhibit 3 

## **HKD, SGD 2s10s Curves Are More Stable Than AUD and MYR (Normalized Annual Realized Vol of 2s10s)** 

Given the different drivers of curve slopes in Asia, recent policy tightening across Asia has not surprisingly contributed to a decline in USD-Asia curve correlations over the past 12 months – particularly so for curves where correlations with the US curve have historically been high (MYR, SGD and THB). 

## **The anomalies over the past year…** 

- Correlations appear to have declined slightly more for 5- 10yr curve slopes than for 2-5yr curve slopes, which ought to be more sensitive to CB policy cycles; 

- Betas of 2-5yr and 5-10yr curve slopes to movements in comparable US curve slopes have been higher over the past year, than over the past 5 years.  (Exhibit 4) 

## **…could be a result of:** 

- The strong rally in US rates from late-April onwards, which also fed through into strong rallies in many Asian rates curves; 

- Market expectations for only a gradual and moderate degree of policy tightening across Asia notwithstanding the initiation of (surprise, though delayed) policy tightening by the RBI, BoK, BoT and CBC.
