## **Implied Vols (Relative to Realized Vols) Have Come Off Faster in Asia** 

Market expectations for gradual policy tightening have been reflected in subdued levels of realised and implied swaption vols on 2yr Asian swap rates, which have generally continued to trend sideways despite the rate hikes. 

Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg 

**41** 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

**July 30, 2010 Asia Interest Rate Strategist** 

By contrast, realised vols of 10yr swap rates in Asia declined in line with US until 1Q10, before rebounding sharply in 2Q on the back of the sharp rally in US rates (INR, KRW, THB and TWD). Nevertheless, despite the fall and rise in realized volatilities, implied volatilities as a ratio of 3mth realized volatilities have remained low, specifically in KRW and TWD. (Exhibit 5 and 6) 

## Exhibit 5 

## **AXJ 6m10y Realized Vols Picked Up Post Hikes….** 

- in turn, lower realised rates and curve vols result in flatter curves and – eventually – to declining levels of implied vols. 

## **PCA Analysis Also Shows Less Predictable Curve Behaviour and Extremely Depressed Bellies** 

An analysis of curve behaviour using PCA[1] also indicates less predictable curve behaviour of US and Asian curves, notably: 

- a higher degree of volatility of the various PC-factors – reflecting higher levels of volatility of rates, curve slope and curvature.  Although this volatility was particularly pronounced during 2008-2009, only the PC factors in the SGD and THB curves have thus far converged back towards historical trends. 

- a divergence of PCA factors from historical norms, particularly with regard to absolute yield levels (PC1)[2] and curvature (PC3) notably in USD, HKD. 

Note: The Realized volatility is for 3m Source: Morgan Stanley Research 

## Exhibit 6 

## **….but the Implied Vols (Relative to Realized Vols) Still Continue to Grind Lower, and Faster than US** 

PCA analysis suggest that curvatures are generally too flat (less convex) compared with historical trends[3] , whereas curve slopes are about average considering current low levels of absolute yields and policy rates.  Flat curvature is particularly pronounced in 3-7yr USD, HKD and 3-5yr SGD curve tenors. This mirrors the superior carry and rolldown on 3-7yr USD, HKD and 3-5yr SGD swap tenors in these curves and related butterfly spreads with bellies in these curve areas. 

## **Curvatures Are Not Uniformly ‘Concave’: Bellies Are Still Slightly ‘Cheap’ in TWD and THB** 

However, 3-7yr curve tenors are currently neither uniformly rich, nor are curvatures uniformly flat (less convex) across Asia.  Whereas 3-7yr USD, HKD swap rates are currently rich (Exhibit 9), the opposite is currently the case in THB and TWD (Exhibit 7 and 8).  PCA-weighted butterflies, in which THB 7s and TWD 3s and 5s form the bellies, offer superior carry and rolldown, while being relatively cheap versus their average levels since 2006.  This points to attractive opportunities to receive the bellies of the THB, TWD curves.  By contrast, we believe that flat USD, HKD curvatures are unlikely to correct 

Source: Morgan Stanley Research 

These two markets are – not coincidentally – the two markets with the flattest 5-10yr curve slopes in Asia with strong onshore insurance demand for yield through structured products (TWD Implied Vol/ 3m Realized Vol for 6m10y being 66%, and the same for KRW and US being 89%). Given low and falling yields globally, the low levels of HKD, KRW, TWD realised and of KRW and TWD implied vols in particular may portend similar trends in other markets, i.e. where: 

- low absolute yields lead to declining realised yield and curve volatilities as investors extend down the curve for carry and liability hedging purposes; 

> _1 PCA decomposes historical curve dynamics into common uncorrelated drivers called principal components. Typically, the first 3 principal components (PCs) capture more than 99% of the variance of individual curve tenors as individual curve tenors tend to be correlated to each other – e.g. in terms of direction.  The first 3 PC factors typically denote commonality of behaviour of indidivual curve tenors in terms of direction (PC1), curve slope (PC2) and curve slope (PC3)._ 

> _2 The line representing PC1 shows the number of standard deviations, which swap rates in a given curve currently deviate from average historical swap rates. PC2 and PC3 show the same with regard to curve slopes and curvature._ 

> _3  Our PCA analysis suggests that changes in curvature generally tend to pivot around 3-4yr tenors (with a bias towards 3s) in most curves, except for INR and NZD (2-3yrs)._

**July 30, 2010 Asia Interest Rate Strategist** 

## **Conclusions** 

soon as low absolute yields enhance investor demand for the superior carry and rolldown returns that 3-7yr USD, HKD curve tenors offer. 

- **Asian Curve Regimes fundamentally different from the US** : US and Asian curve regimes have been fundamentally different since January 2009 compared with those in the preceding five years. Moreover, Asian curves – except the HKD and TWD curves – have been behaving differently versus the USD curve in both 2-5yr and 5-10yr curve tenors.

- **Long-end volatilies suggest uncertain growth outlooks:** Higher levels of rate and curve volatility suggest that curves have become unhinged in the absence of central bank policy guidance and given an uncertain economic outlook. This has been particularly true for long-end rates, whereas (surprise) policy tightening has had little discernible impact on front-end Asian rates (contrary to front-end USD rates when the Fed started hiking rates in 1994).

## **HKD 2s7s10s (2* Belly – Wings)** 

- **Low front-end vols imply low rate move expectations:** Low implied and realised vols in front-end rates across Asia mirror market expectations for a gradual and moderate pace of policy tightening across the region (except in INR). In turn, receiving on upticks in front-end rates is recommended. 

- **Divergence in PC factors explains the new Regime:** PCA analysis indicates that curvature is historically low in USD, HKD and SGD (in case of 3s).  Although PCA analysis usually points to mean reversion opportunities when curve slopes and particularly butterfly spreads diverge significantly from their historical means, the less convex nature of USD, HKD and SGD butterflies may mirror superior carry and rolldown returns in 3-7yr curve tenors in an environment where carry and rolldown returns are becoming increasingly important. 

- **Curvatures could remain depressed:** Moreover, curvature may remain more depressed given: a) a subdued and sub-trend global growth outlook, b) global deleveraging – this will spur increased bank and investor demand for carry and rolldown in bonds and swaps up to 5yrs, as was seen in Asia post the 1998 crisis, c) a general lack of corporate paying interest as long as curve slopes remain steep.

- **The outliers: THB, TWD bellies are relatively cheap:** In contrast to the richness of USD, HKD and SGD curve 

**43** 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

**July 30, 2010 Asia Interest Rate Strategist** 

bellies, the bellies of the THB, TWD curves (THB 7s and TWD 3s and 5s) are still relatively cheap despite superior carry and rolldown returns.  In part, this reflects flush money market liquidity, liability hedging in longer swap tenors and a risk premium for corporate hedging flows. However, the richness of THB 5s and 7s and TWD 3s and 5s offer good carry opportunities through butterflies or on a forward-forward spread basis, in our opinion. 

- **Richness in 3-5yr tenors offer opportunities in 5-7yr cash bonds:** For other curves, where 3s and 5s are relatively rich, receiving into relatively rich curve tenors could potentially generate superior carry and rolldown returns.  However, our PCA-analysis suggests that flat curvature (and richness of butterfly bellies) tends to be seen in 3-7yr curve tenors.  That said, 5-7yr cash bond tenors do offer superior carry and rolldown in e.g. HK EFN, Singapore SGS and Malaysian bonds. This reflects the fact that bonds up to 5 years are structurally expensive in these markets due to the recycling of surplus liquidity by local banks into cash bonds. 

## **Curve Views and Trade Ideas** 

## **Australia** 

**Curve View:** The AUD IRS curve has traditionally seen bear flattening and bull steepening moves, and the curve tends to steepen aggressively once the RBA cuts policy rates. Risks of rising household mortgage rates, property weakness and a scaling back of bank lending could lead the market to price in more RBA policy easing (Australian banks: Supply Deluge, 9 July 2010), particularly following soft Q2 2010 CPI data.  In turn, we lean towards a bull steepening bias in AUD IRS, notwithstanding investor interest to receive long-end rates for yield pick-up. (Exhibit 10) 

## **Trade ideas:** 

- Receive AUD 1yr1yr IRS @ 5.25%, targeting 4.90%, stoploss: 5.40% (conditional entry: 5 July 2010, hit: 27 July 2010) 

- Receive AUD 5yr5yr @ 6.30% (conditional entry: 30 July), target: 5.50% (stop-loss: 6.45%) 

_Risks:_ A further pick-up in economic growth or rising inflation forces the RBA to raise rates 

## **China** 

**Curve View** _:_ regression analysis indicates a high degree of correlation between USD and CNY 2-5yr curve slopes over the past year – contrary to trends over the past five years. (Exhibit 11). This may be coincidental as US/global/China slowdown fears and Chinese policy tightening coincided during Q2 2010.  With the Chinese authorities signaling growth as a greater policy priority, which should lead to an easier PBOC policy stance from Q4 2010 onwards, we believe the CNY (ND)IRS curve will bear steepen independently of the USD curve (China: CNY IRS Bear Steepeners Preferred, 23 July 2010). 

## **Trade Ideas:** 

- 2-5yr steepener (entry: 53bp, target: 70bp, stop-loss: 35bp, preferred entry: 40bp) 

_Risks:_ further stop-loss liquidation of pay IRS positions on sharper than expected economic slowdown and rising inflation.

## **India** 

**Curve View** _:_ we expect the high correlation between 2-5yr USD IRS and INR OIS curve slopes over the past year to break down given prospects for further bear OIS curve flattening as RBI has indicated that: a) liquidity conditions will remain tight, which should result in the O/N rate trading at/above the RBI’s repo rate, except for a modest liquidity surplus during early August, b) further policy tightening – possibly as early as 16 September. A further rise in 5yr INR OIS will, however, be mainly driven by higher front-end rates in the absence of major supply.  In turn, this merits tactical 5yr OIS receive positions at higher levels (see below); however, in the near-term, we recommend taking advantage of any dip in OIS rates on easing liquidity in early to pay rates on dip. 

## **Trade Ideas:** 

- INR OIS 2-5yr curve flatteners on any corrective steepening in early August (entry: 67bp, target: 25bp, stop-loss: 80bp) 

- Pay 5yr OIS outright until the 1yr4yr forward rate reaches 8.8% or versus long 10yr GOI positions 

_Risks:_ money market liquidity remains in surplus in excess of INR200bn or beyond mid-August; 10yr GOI yields underperform 5yr INR OIS in the event of large-scale bank liquidation of GOI holdings to fund credit growth. 

**Curve View** _:_ although we don’t preclude a front-loading of BoK policy tightening with further rate hikes in either August or September, the BoK may find it difficult to justify raising rates much above 3% given weakness in property prices and in view of the government’s pro-growth policy stance (note: MS economics expect further policy tightening to 3.50% over 12mths). In turn, we recommend receiving 9x12mth FRAs and 1yr1yr forwards on any upticks.  At the long end, strong institutional demand for KTBs should cap 10yr KRW IRS, though the 2-5yr KRW IRS curve may flatten modestly further on 5yr KRW CCS receiving flows.  However, a lack of gamma hedging flows on spread range accrual products should preclude major further curve flattening for an overall neutral curve view. 

## **Trade Ideas:** 

- sell 9x12mth FRAs on upticks to 3.70%, targeting 3.35%, stop-loss: BoK hikes rates to 3% before year-end 

- pay 10yr KRW IRS, long 10yr KTBs (entry: 4.30%/4.99% on 2 July, target: 4.20%/ 4.50%, stop-loss: 4.35%/5.20%) 

- _Risks:_ BoK hikes rates at a more aggressive pace towards 

- 3.50% within the scope of 9 months. 

## **Singapore** 

**Curve View** _:_ the correlation between SGD and USD curve slopes has been surprisingly low over the past year. We attribute this to the failure of SGD 2-5yr and 5-10yr curve spreads to follow USD bull curve flattening, with consistent steepening in the SGD 5-10yr curve slope also reflecting sizable anticipated corporate liability hedging flows.  Although timing of these liability flows is uncertain, 5yr5yr forward SGD receive positions offer the best Beta-adjusted rolldown within the region and may be warranted at 3.60%.

## **Malaysia/Taiwan/Thailand** 

**Curve View** _:_ the TWD and THB curves are the two curves in Asia where 2-5yr curve slopes look too steep, and 5s too cheap versus 2s and 10s. Consequently, we recommended receiving 2yr3yr TWD, THB (and MYR) versus pay positions in 5yr5yr forwards on 10 June 2010 _(AXJ Trading Strategies: Trade ideas to position for higher rates_ ).  These positions anticipated further bull flattening in 2s5s, limited further downside in 5yr5yr forwards, while offering attractive carry and rolldown. 

## Exhibit 14 

**THB, TWD, MYR 2s5s Are Relatively Steep Compared to Past Five Years  (2y3y vs. 5y5y spreads)** 

## **2y3y v. 5y5y (THB, TWD and MYR)** 

Of the 3 positions, MYR 5s have richened to fair value, whereas THB 5yr5yr, TWD forwards have declined further (though equal to TWD2yr3yr forward rates) Exhibit 14). Although TWD 5yr5yr forwards continue to be weighed down by onshore insurer receiving interest, and THB5yr5yr forwards are benefiting from benign TGB issuance prospects, we do expect THB, TWD 5yr5yr forwards to rise again on any sharp rise in long end USD rates. 

Meanwhile, the front-ends of all 3 curves are not pricing in significant further BNM, CBC, BOT policy tightening. In turn, we see scope for a further flattening of TWD 2-5yr and TWD25yr curve slopes in excess of the negative carry on these positions (note: the 6mth THB SOR swap fixing rate has recently started to rise from sub-BOT policy rate levels).

**47** 

## **M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

## **July 30, 2010 Asia Interest Rate Strategist** 

## **What’s Moved?** 

## **What’s Moved** 

- **The Asian Swaps had a mixed performance over the week, with the exception of India, where the front end sold off heavily post hike. 1y IRS selling off the most (35bps), and Australia/New Zealand front end rallying th most (8-11bps).** 

- **The Curves saw the steepening in most of the countries, mostly bull steepening, with the exception of India and Thailand, where the curves bear flattened.** 

- **INR 1y IRS has sold off by approximately 138bps in the past three months, diverging from the rest of the region, driven by the rate hikes and the tighter liquidity. Similarly, INR 1s5s flattened by 100bps over the past three months.** 

- **Just as INR font ends sold off heavily, HKD, NZD and SGD front ends rallied strongly, with 2y1y and 3y1y rallying the most over the past three months. NZD 3y1y rallied by 82bps over the past three months, and HKD 2y1y rallied by 88.2bps** 

## **Most Extreme** 

- **As the fixed income markes remain in a sticky mode in the developed world, two Asian markets, MYR and INR, stand out as the most extreme (positive ZScore of swaps). INR 2y and MYR 1y have one year ZScores of 3.71 and 1.89 respectively.** 

- **Except for MYR and INR, the NZD, SGD, and HKD 2y and 3y forwards remain at quite low levels, with NZD 3y5y at 5.66% being the richest forward in AXJ, with a 1y ZScore of -2.67** 

- **As the 5y point remains rich on the curves, almost across the globe, the butterflies stand out at almost historical extremes. The forward spreads like SGD 2y forward 3s5s or HKD 3y forward 3s5s stand at near-extreme levels.** 

- **Similarly, the 1y2y  vs. 5y in HKD (or a 1s3s5s fly) and 1y1y vs. 5y in KRW (or a 1s2s5s fly) stand as the two most extreme (positive) ZScore spreads in Asia.** 

## **Best Carry** 

- **Despite all the rallies so far, front ends still remain attractive in Asia, from a carry and Rolldown perspective, though not as juicy as the beginning of the year.** 

- **THB 1y offers the best 3m carry and Rolldown still remain the best in Asia on an outright as well as on a volatility adjusted scale.** 

- **On an outright carry scale, even KRW and the NZD 1y IRS rank amongst the top three, with 3m carry and Rolldown of 29bps and 28bps respectively.** 

- **In the Curves Space, Korean Spots as well as the forward curves have the best Sharpe Ratio, and have consistently been a great Sharpe Ratio over the past few months.** 

- **KRW spot 2s3s, 1y forward 1s2s and spot 2s5s rank amongst the best three on a volatility-adjusted carry Rolldown scale. (3m vol adjusted carry ~ 1.6)** 

- **AUD forward 1s2s are the outlier in the region, with only curves which show the negative Rolldown of forward 1s2s.**

## **Analyst Stock Ratings** 

Overweight (O or Over) - The stock's total return is expected to exceed the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months. 

Equal-weight (E or Equal) - The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index, on a riskadjusted basis over the next 12-18 months. 

Not-Rated (NR) - Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the relevant country MSCI Index on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. 

Underweight (U or Under) - The stock's total return is expected to be below the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. 

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months. 

## **Analyst Industry Views** 

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index.

## **EM Profile** Hiking Cycles and Flattening Trends in CEEMEA 

## Exhibit 1 

## **Statistics of Previous Hiking Cycles in CEEMEA** 

As we approach the start of the hiking cycles in CEEMEA, the yield curves tend to flatten. Looking back at history, the swap curves started flattening before the delivery of the hikes, and most continued doing so through the hiking cycle. **We suggest accumulating flattener positions in Turkey as we approach the hiking cycle in 1Q11.** 

**Czech Republic:** The curve started to flatten 5-7 months before the first hike. Our economists expect the first hike (25bp) to be delivered in 2Q11. Assuming the curve behaves like it did during the second hiking cycle (i.e., flattening 7 months before the first hike), we believe that investors should consider putting on flatteners towards the end of this year. 

**Hungary:** The curve started to flatten convincingly around two months before the first hike. With the first 25bp hike forecast to be delivered in 3Q11, it is still too early to position for flatteners in Hungary, in our view.

**Israel:** During both the (relatively short) hiking cycles, the curve started flattening 1-2 months before the delivery of the first hike. What is interesting, however, is that most of the flattening happened before the delivery of the hikes. 

**Poland:** The curve started steepening 3-5 months before the first hike, with 2s5s flattening around 100bp and 2s10s flattening by 130bp during the previous two hiking cycles. 

**South Africa:** There is a big discrepancy in the timing of the flattening in the previous two hiking cycles. Our economists forecast 100bp of hikes in 2011, with the first 50bp hike in 2Q11. We think that it is still too early to be positioned in flatteners now. Any flattening in the near term will most likely be driven by positive risk sentiment driving duration-buying at the mid-to-long end of the curve. 

**Turkey:** In the previous two (short) hiking cycles, the curve flattened 1-2 months before the first hike. Going forward, we would recommend fading any steepening moves, and start accumulating flattener positions as we approach the start of the hiking cycle in 1Q11.

**July 14, 2010 EM Profile – Hiking Cycles and Flattening Trends in CEEMEA** 

## **EM Profile: Hiking Cycles and Flattening Trends in CEEMEA** 

## **The Next Big Trend – Curve Flattening?** 

Since 2008, as most countries in CEEMEA were experiencing growth slowdown and recession, central banks in this region have embarked on deep rate-cutting cycles. Yield curves bullsteepened on the back of that, and our economists now think that the policy rates have most likely bottomed in CEEMEA. In fact, the Bank of Israel (BoI) started hiking rates back in July 2009, as part of its rate normalisation process. 

Most central banks are likely to remain on hold over the next few months, according to our economists’ forecasts. In this environment, the curve shape tends to be dictated by fund flows and risk sentiment: in a positive risk environment, inflows are normally directed to the mid-to-long end of the curve, particularly if there is no major mispricing in the front end of the curves. The opposite generally holds true in a riskoff environment as well. 

## **As we approach the start of the hiking cycles in CEEMEA, the yield curves tend to flatten in anticipation of a) higher policy (and hence front-end) rates; as well as b) lower inflation expectations in the future, and hence lower expected future short-term interest rates.** 

Market expectations about future economic activity and risk premium play a role as well. A large part of risk premium is compensation for the uncertainty about the future direction of monetary policy. As long as the markets are uncertain when the hiking cycle is going to commence, a non-trivial part of the high long-term rates is due to risk premia. When the consensus is formed about the start of the hiking cycle, or when the central banks signal that the tightening cycle is imminent, the risk premia can thus begin to collapse. 

## **In fact, looking back at history, the swap curves generally started flattening** _**before**_ **the delivery of the rate hikes, and most continued doing so through the hiking cycle.** 

Indeed, this is already happening in Latin America, with the BRL DI curve flattening as hiking cycles began in this market. A similar analysis has been done by our LatAm colleagues – see _Hiking Cycles and Term Spreads in Latin America_ , Rogerio Oliveira and Juha Seppala, May 26, 2010. 

In order to help us time our entry into flattening trades, we attempt to investigate a) how long before the delivery of the first hike that the swap curve started its flattening trend; and b) how long the flattening trend lasted for.

## **Czech Republic** 

There were two hiking cycles in Czech since 2004 – the first (mini) cycle was between April and September 2004, when the CNB hiked just 50bp. A more prolonged hiking cycle was seen between October 2005 and February 2008, with a cumulative rate hike of 200bp over 28 months. In Exhibits 3 and 4, we show the policy rate paths during the hiking cycles, as well as the evolution of CZK 2s5s and 2s10s spread. 

Unsurprisingly, given the shallow hiking cycle in 2004, the curve flattening was fairly modest as well – 2s5s and 2s10s flattened by 50bp and 80bp (peak to trough), respectively. More interestingly, **the flattening started almost 5 months before the first rate hike** (see Exhibit 5).

## **Czech Republic Hiking Cycle (I)**

In the second hiking cycle, 2s5s and 2s10s flattened by almost 80bp and 150bp, respectively, on the back of 200bp of tightening by the CNB. **The flattening trend started around 7 months before the first rate hike. In fact, the curve was already flattening even before the CNB delivered the final rate cut, as the market prepared for the reversal of the monetary policy cycle.**

It is worth highlighting, however, that the flattening trend was not entirely one-directional. There were three episodes of steepening during the hiking cycle – the first two instances happened when the CNB held the policy rate unchanged for 9 months and 7 months, respectively, while the third instance was on the back of the EUR curve steepening.

Our economists are forecasting unchanged rates at 0.75% over the next three quarters. The next 25bp hike is currently forecast to be delivered in 2Q11. **Assuming that the curve behaves like it did during the second hiking cycle (i.e., flattening 7 months before the first rate hike), investors should consider putting on flatteners towards the end of this year.** 

## **Hungary** 

There were also two hiking cycles in Hungary – the first was from June to November 2006 (200bp hike), and the second was a much shallower hiking cycle (100bp), from March to June 2008. We have excluded the emergency hiking cycle in September 2008, where the curve flattened significantly on the back of one 250bp hike, but steepened up almost immediately after that. 

During the first hiking cycle, 2s5s and 2s10s flattened by 70bp and 120bp, respectively. 2s5s started flattening approximately 4 months before the first rate hike, but 2s10s only started flattening convincingly around 2 months before the first hike.

## **Israel** 

There were also two relatively short hiking cycles in Israel – 75bp hike between July 2007 and January 2008, and 100bp hike between May and September 2008. More recently, the BoI started hiking rates since July 2009 (100bp so far), and our economists are forecasting another 200bp of hikes over the next 18 months. 

**During both earlier hiking cycles, the curve started flattening 1-2 months before the delivery of the first hike.** 2s5s flattened (peak to trough) by 30bp in the first hiking cycle, and by 50bp in the second cycle – both by approximately 50% of the cumulative amount of hikes. 2s10s flattened by more – 40bp in the first hiking cycle and 70bp in the second hiking cycle, or approximately 70% of the cumulative rate hikes. 

What is interesting, however, is that **most of the flattening happened** _**before**_ **the delivery of the rate hikes** (see Exhibits 9-11). In fact, during hiking cycle (I), the curve retraced almost all of the flattening after the delivery of the first rate hike. This could be due to the efficient, forward-looking signaling from the BoI, and therefore allowing the curve to adjust very quickly during the turns of the policy rate cycles.

## **Poland** 

Since 2004, Poland has seen two hiking cycles as well. The first one was relatively short (125bp over three months – June to September 2004), with peak-to-trough flattening of 105bp for 2s5s and 130bp for 2s10s. The curve started flattening around 5 months before the first hike, and continued to do so until the last hike was delivered. 

One possible reason why the curve flattened way before the first hike was due to the sell-off in 2003/early 2004, pushing the curve steeper. As risk sentiments improved, the curve also started to flatten, even before the NBP started hiking rates in June 2004.

## **Poland Hiking Cycle (I)**

The second hiking cycle lasted for 15 months (April 2007 to July 2008), where the NBP hiked 200bp. Despite the deeper hiking cycle, 2s5s and 2s10s flattened by the same amount as the first hiking cycle. **The pace of the flattening was much more gradual than in the first hiking cycle, starting at around 3 months before the first hike.**

## **South Africa** 

During the first hiking cycle (January-September 2002), the SARB hiked 400bp over 8 months, at 100bp per meeting. The curve started to flatten convincingly a month prior to the first hike, with a peak-to-trough flattening of 170bp and 250bp for 2s5s and 2s10s, respectively. On average, the flattening was around 20bp/month for 2s5s, and 27bp/month for 2s10s.

The second hiking cycle was much longer, spanning over 25 months (June 2006 to July 2008), with a more gradual, 50bpper-meeting hikes. The flattening trend started much earlier, almost 13 months before the first hike, and continued until after the penultimate hike in November 2007 (possibly because the market thought that was the last hike). 2s5s and 2s10s flattened by 180bp and 290bp, or roughly 6bp/month and 10bp/month, respectively.

## **Turkey** 

The previous two hiking cycles only lasted for 2-3 months. The first was in fact an emergency rate hike in June 2006, when the market was selling off (USD/TRY spiked from 1.32 to 1.70 within a month). The CBT reacted by hiking 175bp and 225bp in two meetings within the same month. When the market settled down thereafter, the CBT hiked by another 25bp in July 2006, and that marked the end of the hiking cycle. Unsurprisingly, the curve started flattening just before the rate hikes, with 2s5s and 2s10s flattening by 230bp and 400bp, respectively. 

Exhibit 18 

Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg 

**Given the huge differences between the two hiking cycles, timing the flattening trend is harder to call in South Africa.** Our economists are forecasting 100bp of hikes in 2011, with the first 50bp hike in 2Q11. Unlike the previous two cycles, the SARB is unlikely to embark on a prolonged monetary tightening cycle, we believe, especially with inflation expected to stay within the 3-6% target range over the next 18 months. **We therefore think that it is still too early to be positioned in flatteners now. Any flattening in the near term will most likely be driven by positive risk sentiments driving duration-buying at the mid-to-long end of the curve.** 

Exhibit 17

## **Turkey Hiking Cycle (I) – Emergency Hikes** 

**==> picture [183 x 107] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
18.0 50<br>17.5 0<br>17.0 -50<br>16.5 -100<br>-150<br>16.0<br>TU Repo -200<br>15.5 TRY 2s5s -250<br>15.0 TRY 2s10s -300<br>14.5 -350<br>14.0 -400<br>13.5 -450<br>13.0 -500<br>05/06 05/06 06/06 06/06 06/06 06/06 06/06 07/06 07/06 07/06<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg 

The second hiking cycle started in May 2008, when the CBT hiked 50bp. This was followed by another two 50bp hikes in the two subsequent months, and the hiking cycle was cut short by the sell-off in September 2008. Interestingly, despite USD/TRY moving from 1.15 to 1.69 during the sell-off, the CBT kept rates on hold, unlike the previous emergency hiking cycle in 2006. 

The curve started flattening 1 month before the first hike, with peak-to-trough flattening of 145bp and 235bp for 2s5s and 2s10s, respectively. Another point worth highlighting is that most of the flattening this time occurred _prior_ to the first hike (see Exhibits 19-20), as the market priced in significant amounts of rate hikes very quickly even before the CBT started hiking rates.

## Exhibit 19 

## **Turkey Hiking Cycle (II)** 

**==> picture [197 x 107] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
17.00 TU Repo 0<br>16.75 TRY 2s5s<br>TRY 2s10s -50<br>16.50<br>-100<br>16.25<br>16.00 -150<br>15.75<br>-200<br>15.50<br>-250<br>15.25<br>15.00 -300<br>03/08 03/08 04/08 04/08 05/08 05/08 06/08 06/08 07/08 07/08<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg 

Exhibit 20 

Based purely on the timing of the rate hiking cycle, we may not be far from initiating flattening trades in Turkey, as our economists are expecting the rate hike to start in 1Q11. The recent flattening happened because the market removed some inflation premium further out on the curve, as inflation expectations continued to inch lower. At the same time, the continued improvement in Turkey’s fiscal outlook also supports the argument for a flatter curve. 

Given the sound fundamental picture and light foreign positioning in local bonds, coupled with TRY real rates being among the highest in the CEEMEA region, we think it is possible that the recent flattening momentum could continue in the near term. **Going forward, we would recommend fading any steepening moves, and starting to accumulate flattener positions as we approach the start of the hiking cycle, possibly in 1Q11.**

May 26, 2010 

**Global Global** 

**EM Profile** Hiking Cycles and Term Spreads in Latin America 

**We study the dynamics of Latin America interest rate curves around the inflection period when the monetary authorities start a tightening cycle. We find some evidence which suggests curve flatteners as good risk/reward exposures near the infection points.** 

In this piece, we take a look at the behavior of term spreads of some of the Latin America local interest rates curves when monetary policy shifts from neutral to hiking mode. 

A serious challenge for any strong conclusion is the region’s relatively recent macro stabilization and use of monetary policy tools. Indeed, the data contain only two full hiking cycles in Brazil and only one hiking cycle in Chile and Mexico. The current exchange rate regime in Brazil dates from 1999, Chile started using the nominal overnight rate as a monetary policy instrument only in 2001 and Mexico formally implemented its inflation-targeting strategy only in 1999. Also, developed interest rate curves for all these countries only date from the early 2000s. Hence, given the short time series of relevant data in Latin American countries, we also look at the empirical and theoretical evidence concerning the US to suggest patterns. 

Although inconclusive from a strict econometric point of view, the results from the historical data analysis suggest that curve-flattener exposures tend to exhibit solid risk/rewards near the infection points. 

Taking as reference the 1s10s slope, Brazil’s DI curve experienced on average about 10bp of flattening per week in the months before and after the hiking cycle started. The developments are a bit more mixed and less pronounced for the other countries, with the Chile Camara curve materially flattening for several months after the first hike and the slope of the Mexican TIIE curve gradually reducing as the tightening cycle approached.  The differences between the countries in how fast the curve responds to anticipated changes in monetary policy can probably be accounted by differences in the central banks’ credibility and differences in response to outside influences (i.e., Fed policy).

## **EM Profile: Hiking Cycles and Term Spreads in Latin America** 

Rogerio Oliveira +1 (212) 761-1204 / +55 11 3048-6302 Juha Seppala +1 (212) 761-1949 

## **Monetary Policy in Latin America** 

The end of the easing cycle and the beginning of a hiking cycle has either arrived or will arrive soon to the three major Latin American economies. Brazil’s central bank raised its overnight Selic rate by 75bp at the end of April and Morgan Stanley expects Chile and Mexico to start raising overnight rates in June 2010 and March 2011, respectively. Given that the interest rate curves can be decomposed as the sum expected future short-term interest rates and risk premia (minus a small option value of convexity, which we ignore in this article), it is valuable to understand what this view of future monetary policy paths implies to the curve trades. 

The biggest problem in analyzing the relationship between monetary policy and term spreads in Latin American countries is the relatively recent stabilization of markets and policy in these countries. The current exchange rate regime in Brazil dates from 1999. Similarly, Chile started using the nominal overnight rate as a monetary policy instrument in 2001 and Mexico formally implemented its inflation-targeting strategy in 1999. Finally, and most importantly, the Camara swap market in Chile and the TIIE swap market in Mexico only date from early 2000s. 

In our analysis, we use monthly data from January 2004. Given the short time series of relevant data in Latin American countries, it is worthwhile to discuss the empirical and theoretical evidence concerning the US. 

## **Monetary Policy and the Yield Curve in the US** 

Every recession in the US since the early 1950s (that is, since the establishment of well-functioning government bond markets) has been predicted by an inverted (downwardsloping) yield curve with only one false signal.[1] The yield curve is the steepest at the bottom of the business cycle and the most inverted at the top of the business cycle. 

The precise mechanism which causes this regularity is not known, but clearly the Fed’s policy plays an important role. At the top of the cycle, the Fed is concerned about an overheated economy, which results in slowing activity, which results in lower expectations of future inflation and hence lower expected future short-term interest rates. 

Market expectations about future economic activity and the risk premium play a role as well.[2] It is worth emphasizing that a large part of risk premium is compensation for the uncertainty about the future direction of monetary policy. As long the markets are uncertain when the hiking cycle is going to start, a non-trivial part of the high long-term rates is due to risk premia. When the consensus is formed about the beginning of the hiking cycle, the risk premia can begin to collapse. 

Summarizing, based on the US evidence, we should expect the approaching hiking cycle to signal flattening term spreads. Testing this hypothesis empirically is not easy as the data contain only two full hiking cycles in Brazil and only one hiking cycle in Chile and Mexico. Therefore, in the subsequent analysis, we limit ourselves to graphical evidence, which turns out to be quite revealing. 

## **Brazil** 

In Exhibit 1 we present PRExDI swap curve term spreads for different maturities and the overnight Selic target rate. Simple eyeballing the data reveals significant negative correlation between the two series. As a matter of fact, the correlations vary between -0.67 for the 1s5s spread and -0.51 for the 2s10s spread. Moreover, it is very informative to look at cumulative changes in term spreads relative to the level at the end the first hiking month. Exhibit 2 shows the average differences for each month five months before to five months after the beginning of the hiking cycle relative to the level at the beginning of the cycle.[3] 

> 1See _The Yield Curve as a Leading Indicator: Frequently Asked Questions_ by Arturo Estrella, October 2005, available at www.newyorkfed.org/research/capital_markets/ycfaq.pdf for a long list of references. 

> 2See _Monetary Policy and Rejections of the Expectations Hypothesis_ by Federico Ravenna and Juha Seppala, May 2007, available at http://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=923779 for a theoretical model able to capture this mechanism. 

> 3The hiking cycles begin in November 2004, April 2008, and April 2010. The contribution of the last hike is used only for the months before the beginning of the cycle.

Mexico differs from Brazil and Chile as in the previous hiking cycle flattening began 12 months before the beginning of the cycle and ended already one month after the beginning of the tightening cycle.  Drawing conclusions about Mexico is complicated by the fact that Mexico’s rate moves have traditionally been correlated with the moves by the Fed in the US. 

## **Potential Trade Opportunities** 

The empirical evidence presented in the previous sections, _conditional_ on hiking cycles starting as expected, leads to straightforward trade recommendations: once one is as _certain_ as possible about when the hiking cycle is going to start, one should initiate curve-flattener trades. The key words here are, of course, _conditional_ and _certain_ . The above analysis and recommendations rely on assumptions that (in the case of Chile and Mexico) we know when the hiking cycle is going to start or (in the case of Brazil) once the hiking cycle starts it continues in a predictable fashion as it has done in the past. 

The recent turmoil in global markets and general riskreduction taking place due to euro area sovereign risks, China growth concerns, and financial sector risks may invalidate the above recommendations.  If there is a danger that LatAm will experience a double-dip recession, the hiking cycle will be put on hold or maybe even replaced by an easing cycle.[4] And, as a matter of fact, compared to the situation four weeks ago, both in Brazil and Mexico (but not in Chile), term spreads have started to steepen again. Given the increased uncertainty and its effect on long-term premium (which tends to increase rates), this is hardly surprising. Investors interested in LatAm rates should then carefully monitor the situation in Brazil and Mexico and consider DV01-neutral flattener trades in Chile. 

In the case of Brazil, the curve has already flattened quite a lot, but, since the beginning of the recent turmoil, has started to steepen again. We therefore wait for a better entry point. In the case of Mexico, according to our economists, the beginning of the hiking cycle is still 10 months ahead. Given the global uncertainty, we are currently not inclined to initiate flatteners. However, in the case of Chile, we have a strong conviction about the hiking cycle starting next month. 

Moreover, the term spreads in Chile are at historical all-time highs and there is broad optimism regarding the prospects for Chile’s economy, despite the tragic earthquake of February 27 and its effect on economic activity.  This positive view is based mainly on: (i) the boost to activity from the upcoming rebuilding efforts; (ii) strong momentum in consumption and investment; and (iii) benefits from the ongoing global cyclical rebound.  As a matter of fact, the risk in Chile it is that it may need to hike at a faster pace than the market expects by year-end. 

> 4With a possible exception of Chile, where the overnight rate is about as low as it can be.

## **EM Profile** 

## How to Value Brazil Rates? 

**We present a macro-financial model for fair risk premia and apply it to the Brazil DI curve. Back-testing of our models indicates a success rate in the range of 70% in predicting interest rate movements. Although flatteners have been profitable for most of 2010, the model suggests it is now time switch to steepeners.** 

Nominal interest rates can be decomposed into a compensation for postponing the use of funds (real interest rate), a compensation for the variation in the value of nominal balances (expected inflation), a compensation due to uncertainty about variations in real interest rates and inflation during the holding period (risk premia)[1] . 

Alternatively, local nominal curves can be expressed as a sum of the expected path of the nominal short-term interest rates, which are commonly forecast by economists, plus fair risk premia, whose computations are far from being a trivial exercise, and have increasing importance for longer interest rate tenors. Hence, interest rates strategy depends crucially on good assessment of how much investors are willing to receive, on average, as compensation for risk. 

To give an idea of the performance of our model, Exhibit 1 displays the ex-ante predictions and ex-post realized rates for the DI Jan’11 contract. The model predicted correctly the changes in the DI rate in 10 out of 14 months. We view this result quite respectable. 

Exhibit 2 summarizes our model’s current fair value estimates. The short tenors, up to Jan’12 contract, are cheap while the longer tenors, up to Jan’22 contract, are rich. Hence, the flatteners, which have been very profitable trades during the first half of this year, should now be replaced by steepeners. Interestingly, DI Jan’11xJan17 spread, presented below in Exhibit 8, shows signs of reaching a tipping point at the end of June. The attractiveness of steepeners is based on both risk premia and expected monetary policy.

**July 29, 2010 EM Profile – How to Value Brazil Rates?** 

## **EM Profile: How to Value Brazil Rates?** 

## **The Model** 

The objective of our econometric macro-finance model is to carve out a measure of fair risk premia, i.e. a measure of the compensation for risk, given some observed (and forecast) values of a set of macro-finance fundamentals. 

Extensive literature deals with the term structure of interest rates and calculations of the risk premia for developed economies, principally for the US. The risk premia models range from purely econometric affine models, which use unobservable latent factors, to fully fledged general equilibrium macroeconomic models, which explain the yield curve with specific relationships between observable macroeconomic fundamentals.[2] 

Our model follows a middle way approach by establishing an econometric relationship between the risk premia and a set of relevant financial and macroeconomic variables. The approach is flexible enough to capture idiosyncratic characteristics of Brazil and macro-finance fundamentals without too much rigidity typical of theoretical models. 

We begin by obtaining a crude measure of empirical (observable) ‘forward’ risk premia as the difference between overnight forward rates and expected overnight rates. The latter are extracted from the Brazilian central bank’s surveys of economic forecasts, available in its web page. By integrating these forward risk premia, we can obtain ‘spot’ risk premia, the respective measure for spot prices. 

The next step consists of estimating the statistical relationship between the observed risk premium and a set of financial and macroeconomic fundamentals. These explanatory variables span the set of factors that we have found to be relevant to explain the behavior of the risk premia. Some variables reflect global financial conditions (among them VIX and EM CDX), others capture idiosyncratic aspects of the country of interest (eg, FX volatility and sovereign CDS spreads), while others capture macroeconomic conditions (among them activity or production indexes, and different price indices). 

After this, we use the predictions of the model to compute the fair risk premium based on observed and forecast values of the fundamentals. As such, our model can be used not only to determine the current measure of fair risk premium, but also 

> 2 For a review of the former, please see Monika Piazzesi “Affine Term Structure models,” in the _Handbook of Financial Econometrics_ , volume 1, 2009. For examples of the latter applied to nominal term structure, please see Federico Ravenna and Juha Seppala “ _Monetary Policy and Rejections of Expectations Hypothesis_ ,” 2007 and “ _Monetary Policy, Expected Inflation, and Inflation Risk Premium,_ ’”2007. 

to forecast the dynamic evolution of this premium in the short term. 

Finally, we combine our fair risk premium estimates with Selic rates expectations rate to price all available DI contracts in Brazil. However, consensus expectations are only used for 2012 and beyond. For 2010 and 2011 we use the Selic path forecasted by Morgan Stanley’s economists. We summarize our results above in Exhibit 2. 

Without entering into a discussion of technical details, one final point concerning the treatment of the curves is worth mentioning. To obtain well-behaved spot and forward curves, monetary policy expectations, and risk premia, we apply a smoothing procedure. This guarantees that the obtained risk premium does not adopt counterintuitive shapes and reduces possible measure errors in market prices or estimates of market expectations.[3] Exhibit 3 illustrates how we find empirical forward risk premia. Notice how the risk premia are negative when the expected Selic is above the O/N forward rate.

## **Historical Behavior of the Empirical Risk Premium** 

Exhibit 4 displays the dynamics of spot 3y risk premia and policy rate in Brazil. The risk premium tends to increase when the target rate is on hold between the end of the cutting cycle and the start of the hiking cycle. When hikes begin (or, more precisely, when uncertainty about when they will start declines) risk premia collapse very fast. Moreover, disregarding the spike of October 2008, they tend to be the

Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley, Central Bank of Brazil. 

Our _EM Profile: Hiking Cycles and Term Spreads in Latin America_ (May 26, 2010) addressed the behavior of the term spreads in the major Latin American countries around the beginning of hiking cycles. Our result was that the curve flatteners were attractive risk-reward trades in the months immediately before and after the month when central banks began their hiking cycles. The collapse in risk premium identified in the previous paragraph is one major factor behind this pattern. 

The risk premia are currently historically at very low levels. Given the uncertainty associated with forthcoming Presidential elections and the Central Bank’s decision (maybe prematurely) to slow down the hiking cycle, we suspect that the risk premia will be driven upwards in the near future. 

Exhibit 5 shows the average differences for each month five months before to five months after the beginning of the hiking cycle relative to the level at the beginning of the cycle.[4] Exhibit 5 clearly documents how the risk premia begin to tighten before the hikes begin and continue to do so for several months afterwards. Typically longer tenors tighten more. 

> 4The hiking cycles begin in November 2004, April 2008, and April 2010. The contribution of the last hike is used only for the months before the beginning of the cycle.

## **Historical Back Test** 

Exhibit 1 and 6 display the ex-ante predictions and ex-post realized rates for the DI Jan’11 and Jan’17 contracts. Shorter tenors depend crucially on overnight interest rate predictions whereas longer tenors are more sensitive to risk premia in addition to long-term expectations. Given the difficulty of forming monetary policy expectations seven years ahead, it should not be a surprise that the success ratio of our backtesting is better for Jan’11 tenor, where the model predicted correctly the changes in the DI rate in 10 out of 14 months. Nonetheless, the success ratio of 8 out of 12 months[5] for Jan’17 is still quite respectable, in our view. 

In particular, the model has been able to predict quite well the tightening of the Jan’17 rate that has taken place during the first half of this year. As we noted above and in our _EM Profile: Hiking Cycles and Term Spreads in Latin America_ (May 26, 2010) the curve flatteners have been attractive riskreward trades in the months immediately before and after the months when central banks begin their hiking cycles. 

A real-time test of the performance of our model was the _Latin America Strategy Update: The Trade Implications of Brazil Selic Rate Decision_ (July 21, 2010), published last week. Our model indicated that if the central bank were to hike 50bp on July 21, the short-end would rally and the long-end would widen, and we saw this outcome between July 21 and July 22. 

> 5 In addition to two months which were inconclusive.

The short tenors, up to Jan’12 contract, are cheap while the longer tenors, up to Jan’22 contract, are rich. Hence, the flatteners, which have been very profitable trades during the first half of this year, should now be replaced by steepeners. Interestingly, DI Jan’11xJan17 spread, presented in Exhibit 8, shows signs of reaching a tipping point at the end of June. 

The attractiveness of steepeners is based on both risk premia and expected monetary policy. The risk premia are currently historically at very low levels. Given the uncertainty associated with forthcoming Presidential elections and the Central Bank’s decision to slow down the hiking cycle, we suspect that the risk premia will be driven upwards in the near future.

## **EM Profile** 

How to Value EMFX Implied Volatility? 

**We present our econometric model to evaluate fair implied volatility across several Emerging Market currencies (EMFX). The model uses historical volatility, VIX, CDS spreads, trade balance, CPI and other macroeconomic variables as explanatory variables and may be thought of as a Macro-Finance enhancement of a dynamic ARCH type model. We back-tested the rich/cheap signals from the model using a simple trading strategy and obtained reasonable Sharpe and Sortino ratios.** 

A view on the fairness of implied volatilities is a key for derivatives trading. Factors affecting implied volatility may include the expected future realized volatility; rising risk aversion, possibly as a result of global related sell-offs; and idiosyncratic risks. To embrace such varied possibilities, we run econometric regressions of 12 weeks ahead changes in implied volatility on several macro and financial variables. 

## Exhibit 1 

## **Current and Fair EMFX Vol Levels According to Our Model** 

|**Currency**|**Current 3m vol**|**Fair 3m vol**|**Rich/Cheap**|
|---|---|---|---|
|BRL|15.97|16.74|5%  Cheap|
|COP|14.47|15.74|9%  Cheap|
|HUF|13.99|15.61|12%  Cheap|
|IDR|11.70|12.52|7%  Cheap|
|INR|10.95|9.45|14%  Rich|
|KRW|16.56|15.73|5%  Rich|
|MXN|13.65|13.21|3%  Rich|
|PHP|9.74|11.22|15%  Cheap|
|PLN|12.43|14.57|17%  Cheap|
|RUB|13.50|13.86|3%  Cheap|
|TRY|12.66|15.15|20%  Cheap|
|ZAR|16.26|20.51|26%  Cheap|



Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley 

We then use the forecasts from the best fitted regression and elaborate a buy/sell signal for a very simple deltahedged derivative trading strategy where simulated P&Ls are computed using the actual changes in implied volatility and time decay of the options. We decided that the trading rules should be as simple and transparent as possible to illustrate how one can use our fair value estimates. In practice, one may want to revise the strategies to increase their performance. 

The calculated Sharpe and Sortino ratios from this simple trading strategy encourage us to adopt our forecasting model as a first indication of the richness or cheapness of market implied volatility. 

According to the model, almost all EMFX volatilities are currently undervalued and a few are overvalued. The largest differences between current and fair values are in ZAR (26% cheap) and TRY (20% cheap).

## **EM Profile: How to Value EMFX Implied Vol?** 

## **The Model** 

Our model relies heavily on econometrics and is based on various time series regressions of 12 weeks ahead changes in 3m at-the-money-forward (ATMF) implied volatility on several different macro and financial variables. Exhibit 2 uses MXN as a baseline example and lists the complete set of all potential regressors. We run independent regressions using weekly data for Brazil, Mexico, Colombia, South Africa, Turkey, Hungary, Poland, Russia, Korea, Philippines, Indonesia and India. 

The regression results for 12 weeks ahead changes in 3m MXN implied volatility regressed on variables observable today are displayed on Exhibit 2. The coefficient of determination, 0.52, suggests an exceptionally good fit for changes so far ahead. The most important explanatory variables are all directly FX related: they are the current implied vol, the past change in historical vol, and the level of FX reserves. Therefore, our model may be seen as an enhancement of an ARCH model where macro and financial variables are added to enrich the analysis and results. 

Exhibit 2 

## **Regression of 3M MXN vol(t+12)-vol(t) (R[2 ] = 0.52)** 

|**Variable**|**Coefficient**|**White S.E.**|**t-Statistic**|
|---|---|---|---|
|Constant|-22.45|4.43|-5.07|
|vol(t)<br>CDS|-1.14<br>1.85|0.14<br>0.78|-8.02<br>2.36|
|VIX|-1.04|0.69|-1.50|
|hvol(t)-hvol(t-12)|2.53|0.40|6.34|
|Dummy for 3m presidential elections<br>Trade Balance|4.61<br>-2.44|1.01<br>0.66|4.57<br>-3.68|
|Industrial Production|-1.70|0.68|-2.49|
|CPI|3.53|0.71|5.01|
|FX Reserve|2.38|0.42|5.68|



Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley 

In Exhibit 3 we display the model forecast and the realized 3m ahead implied volatility. Please note that for each period we re-estimated the model using only the information available during that period. Given the re-estimated model, we then used that period’s observations to project 12 weeks ahead prediction. The model, not surprisingly, had problem with the sharp increase and decrease in volatility during the late 2008 to early 2009 financial crisis. On the other hand, its forecasting performance in less volatile periods such as 2007 and 2010 has been exceptionally good. Exhibit 3 also illustrates how our predicted value can be thought of as a 

long-term fair value for the vol. The fair values are less volatile than the actual series. 

Exhibit 3 

## **Predicted vs. Realized 3m MXN Implied Volatilities. (R[2] = 0.52)**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
50<br>45<br>Realized vol Predicted vol<br>40<br>35<br>30<br>25<br>20<br>15<br>10<br>5<br>0<br>Jun-06 Dec-06 Jun-07 Dec-07 Jun-08 Dec-08 Jun-09 Dec-09<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley 

## **A Simple Trading Strategy to Test the Model** 

To test the model’s forecasting power, we back-tested a simple trading strategy, which is illustrated in Exhibit 4 for the TRY baseline example. The strategy is designed to take advantage of the mean-reversion feature in implied volatility, which is obviously salient from the negative coefficient of vol(t) in Exhibit 2. 

We first calculate the spread difference between our 12 weeks ahead forecast and the current value of 3M ATM forward volatility. Next we calculate the 90 days’ rolling standard deviation of this spread. A buy (sell) signal is triggered whenever the spread goes above (below) one unit of standard deviation. The trade is closed when the spread crosses through zero and changes sign. In addition, we close short positions after 12 weeks. 

**86** 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

**June 28, 2010 EM Profile – How to Value EMFX Implied Vol?** 

## Exhibit 4 

## **TRY FX Vol Spread and Current Value** 

**==> picture [262 x 156] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
20<br>Vol Std upper bound Std lower bound Spread Zero<br>100<br>9/26/2008<br>80 10/17/2008 10<br>60<br>0<br>40<br>11/7/2008<br>-10<br>10/10/2008<br>20<br>0 -20<br>Mar- Jul- Nov- Mar- Jul- Nov- Mar- Jul- Nov- Mar- Jul- Nov- Mar- Jul- Nov- Mar-<br>05 05 05 06 06 06 07 07 07 08 08 08 09 09 09 10<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley 

As an example of how to read this graph, notice how the dark blue line (spread) rises above the gray boundary on 9/26/2008. This triggers a buy order. However, two weeks later, on 10/10/2008, the dark blue line is negative (below the zero line). This triggers a sell order and the long position is closed, resulting in an annualized profit of almost 50%. A week later, on 10/17/2008, the dark blue line drops below the gray boundary. This indicates that it makes sense to open a short position. This short position is held for three weeks until the spread becomes positive again (resulting in an annualized profit of 15%). 

We decided that the trading rules should be as simple and transparent as possible to illustrate how one can use our fair value estimates. In practice, one may want to revise the strategies to increase their performance. 

Exhibit 5 displays the details of all the hypothetical TRY volatility trades generated using the trading strategy described above. The return for each trade is calculated by multiplying vega (the sensitivity of the price of the option to changes in volatility) times the change in volatility (from open to close of the trade) and adding the time decay theta (the sensitivity of the price of the option on changes in volatility) times trade horizon. Hence, we are implicitly assuming that options are perfectly delta-hedged. We also annualize the returns.

Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley 

To evaluate the risk-return performance of our trading strategy, we use the returns from the hypothetical trades in Exhibit 4 and compute Sharpe and Sortino ratios (Sortino ratio penalizes only negative returns). These ratios turned out to be solid ones of 0.49 and 2.1, respectively. Only six out of 21 trades produced negative returns, the highest annualized loss being -14%. Moreover, in two out of five losing trades, the loss was generated by onerous time decay of the options and not by incorrect prediction. Clearly one could improve the performance of trading strategies by taking the time decay into account when to close trades. Finally, the last hypothetical trade is currently on so the jury is still out if it will eventually lose money. 

In Exhibit 6, we show the summary statistics of our trading strategy for all 12 currencies. The first statistic, total return, is the sum of annualized returns for all trades. The second measure, Sharpe ratio, may be misleading because the returns are highly skewed to the right: there are few trades that generate high annualized returns such as the 68% return in June 2006 and 50% return in September-October 2008 for TRY.

Based on these results, the model and trading strategy seems to work best for hypothetical trades in BRL, COP, IDR, PHP, RUB, TRY and ZAR. For other currencies such as KRW, MXN and PLN, the results were not as good, although we feel the model might still be useful as a first indication of richness or cheapness of implied volatility.

## **Analyst Stock Ratings** 

Overweight (O or Over) - The stock's total return is expected to exceed the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months. Equal-weight (E or Equal) - The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months. Not-Rated (NR) - Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. Underweight (U or Under) - The stock's total return is expected to be below the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.

## **BRL: Buying FX Inconvertibility Protection** 

**This is an excerpt from the FX Pulse, Defense Systems Go, May 20, 2010.** 

**For important disclosures, refer to the Disclosures Section, located at the end of this report.** 

**92** 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

**May 20, 2010 BRL: Buying FX Inconvertibility Protection** 

## **BRL: Buying FX Inconvertibility Protection** 

Rogerio Oliveira (1 212) 761 1204; (55 11) 3048 6302 Juha Seppala (1 212) 761 1949 

- The new currency laws approved by Venezuela this week bring FX control risks to the spotlight. We elaborate on BRL inconvertibility (tail) risk and possible hedges, which may be more effective than buying Brazil’s CDS and better suited for some investors with onshore assets. 

Brazil is currently one of the favorite destinations for foreign direct and private equity investments. Notwithstanding, investors should be aware that BRL is still a semi-convertible currency. To expatriate funds from Brazil, BRL needs to be converted into USD through a regulated spot FX market. This exposes investors to the (tail) risk of an inconvertibility event where the Brazilian government would keep the official rate artificially strong and impose conversion controls. In this situation, investors may have to endure the loss between the official and the prevailing market USD/BRL exchange rate, which may substantially differ from one another. In the worst case scenario, investors may be unable to convert BRL to USD at any exchange rate. 

## Exhibit 1 

## **Venezuelan Bolivar: Inconvertible Since 2003** 

**==> picture [237 x 144] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
8.00<br>Official Exchange Rate<br>7.00 Official Exchange Rate -- Basic Goods<br>6.00 Market Exchange Rate<br>5.00<br>4.00<br>3.00<br>2.00<br>1.00<br>0.00<br>Jan-02 Jan-03 Jan-04 Jan-05 Jan-06 Jan-07 Jan-08 Jan-09 Jan-10<br>Source: Morgan Stanley, Bloomberg<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


Venezuela is an interesting case study in Latin America as the government imposed capital controls in 2003 and currently there are three coexisting exchange rates, official exchange rate, growing market implied rate, and the official rate for import of food and medicine (Exhibit 1). Most investors can only repatriate money at the parallel ‘market’ exchange rate, which is currently only about half as valuable in USD as the official exchange rate. Earlier this week the Venezuelan government announced a temporary suspension of 

purchase/sale of foreign currency-denominated securities in an attempt to reduce the current wide spread between the two official rates and the parallel rate. We believe that the success of this heterodox policy will probably be limited at best. 

In contrast, in Brazil the local legislation seems to be moving towards making BRL eventually convertible. Nonetheless, there is always a residual risk about the quality of policies of future administrations, and some investors with local exposure should consider protecting their portfolios against the tail risk of an inconvertibility event. The cost of BRL inconvertibility protection is highly correlated with other possible hedges often used by investors such as buying Brazil’s CDS. However, we believe inconvertibility events tend to be more likely and triggered before a default event, as it is highlighted by the case of Venezuela, a country which imposed capital controls but has not defaulted on any of its debt obligations up to this date. Hence, for some investors, particularly for those with FDI and private equity investments onshore in Brazil, inconvertibility swaps may be a more suitable (and sometimes cheaper than CDS) hedging tool. 

## **Inconvertibility Risk** 

In a nutshell, a long BRL inconvertibility protection strategy may be the composition of a long position in USD/BRL through offshore FX forwards (NDF) and a short exposure in USD/BRL through onshore forwards. Exhibit 2 displays the historical data for onshore and offshore USD/BRL forwards. They seem almost identical in most periods (implying an inconvertibility premium close to zero) except in market stress periods when convertibility risks increase and onshore USD/BRL forwards tend to be cheaper than NDF. We have also observed periods when bullish exuberance towards Brazil has pushed the inconvertibility spread into negative territory. 

Offshore and onshore USD/BRL forwards have the same intrinsic values (Forward FX – Strike) /Forward FX and equitable payouts at maturity, unless there is an inconvertibility event and the official FX rates (PTAX) is different from the market set EMTA FX rate. In this case, local forwards would have to be settled by PTAX because of local legislation, whereas offshore NDF are settled by the EMTA rate. This is the basis for the differences between onshore and offshore USD/BRL forward prices. 

**93** 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

**May 20, 2010 FX Pulse** 

## Exhibit 2 

## **Offshore and Onshore 5Y NDF Levels**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
3.2<br>O N-SHO RE NDF O FF-SHO RE NDF<br>3.0<br>2.8<br>2.6<br>2.4<br>2.2<br>\ . J VC av<br>2.0<br>Jan-07 Jul-07 Jan-08 Jul-08 Jan-09 Jul-09 Jan-10<br>Source: Morgan Stanley, Bloomberg<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


## **Hedging with Onshore/Offshore Forwards** 

Onshore forwards are not directly available but they may be synthetically constructed using short-dated FX futures, BRL rates futures (DI curve) and local USD rates futures (CUPOM cambial curve). A proxy for the convertibility spread is the difference between Libor rates and the local USD rates futures that trade in Brazil (CUPOM cambial curve, in Bloomberg type GDA <CRNCY> <CT> <GO>), shown in Exhibit 3. 

Exhibit 3 

## **Inconvertibility Spread versus CDS** 

**==> picture [245 x 137] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
6.0%<br>5Y Inconvertibility Spread<br>5Y CDS Spread<br>5.0%<br>4.0%<br>3.0%<br>2.0%<br>1.0% MN Ms<br>0.0%<br>Oct-06 Apr-07 Nov-07 Jun-08 Dec-08 Jul-09 Jan-10 Aug-10<br>Source: Morgan Stanley, Bloomberg<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


To implement a long protection exposure, investors would have to buy USD/BRL NDF offshore and synthetically short USD/BRL forwards onshore. This entails establishing and managing exposures in the several assets mentioned above, which can be a complex task for many investors. 

Furthermore, the offshore NDF market is not liquid beyond two years. Hence, it may not be feasible to use the forward markets for longer-term hedging to, for example, buy 5Y or 10Y inconvertibility protection and be protected through the following presidential elections in Brazil. 

## **Inconvertibility Swaps** 

The implementation of the hedge can be materially simplified by using Inconvertibility swaps. For tenors beyond 2Y this might be the only viable alternative to clients. Furthermore, as we explain in more detail below, the inconvertibility swap is a more effective hedge than buy/sell offshore/onshore forwards when investors want to protect a fixed USD notional. 

In order to acquire inconvertibility protection through these swaps, the cost for the investors is the convertibility spread, equal to the difference between the CUPOM Cambial (local USD rates) and Libor rates. As long as BRL remains semiconvertible, investors will bear this cost through semi-annual payments. 

The swap is designed to compensate investors for the possible cost of converting onshore BRL into offshore USD in case there is an inconvertibility event and conversion becomes impossible to execute at the official FX rate through customary legal channels in Brazil. Under this scenario, the swap is settled through physical delivery of currencies, with the investor receiving the USD notional amount and paying BRL amount = USD notional x USDBRL official rate. If BRL payment is illegal/impossible, the payment is settled in USD offshore at market FX rate (EMTA). 

## **Comparing Hedging Strategies** 

Exhibit 4 displays the different payouts (in case there is a triggering event) between acquiring inconvertibility protection through the two different hedging strategies (onshore/offshore forwards pair or inconvertibility swap). The first protection is applied to a BRL amount equal to the USD Notional x USD/BRL FX Strike, not to the USD amount. Since a currency devaluation tends to follow an inconvertibility event, part of the protection is likely to be compromised. On the other hand, the inconvertibility protection from the swap is guaranteed to be over the full USD notional amount, and therefore is more effective in a crisis or in a stress scenario. 

Exhibit 4 

**Payout from Different Hedging Strategies** 

|**Transaction**|**Payout Upon Currency Event**|
|---|---|
|InconvertibilitySwap|Notional x(1-PTAX/EMTA)|
|Offshore/Onshore|Notional x NDF Strike x|
|Forwards|(1/Ptax - 1/EMTA)|



Source: Morgan Stanley 

**94** 

**May 20, 2010 FX Pulse** 

## **M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H** 

Further, if the inconvertibility spread increases (CUPOM rates widen in relation to Libor), the mark to market (MTM) gain on the swap is usually higher than on the Onshore/Offshore NDF pair. The inconvertibility spread applies to the entire USD notional of the swap, in contrast to the NDFs where it is only applicable to the intrinsic value. 

Inconvertibility swaps are illiquid and may have material transaction costs for investors who decide to unwind it before maturity. However, the demand for this protection tends to increase substantially at times of market stress, which mitigates the difficulty of unwinding the swap at a premium in a volatile market. 

Furthermore, investors may partially offset the swap exposure and lock in gains by entering into a reverse onshore/offshore forward strategy: selling USD forward offshore and buying USD forwards onshore. Inconvertibility swaps and the onshore/offshore NDF pair do not hedge each other perfectly, 

but the residual exposure may turn out beneficial for the investor if, as would be expected, BRL weakens prior to the inconvertibility event. 

Finally, investors often use CDS as a more liquid instrument to buy protection against a country’s deteriorating fundamentals. Indeed, Exhibit 3 suggests that both the inconvertibility and default risks are highly correlated. However, inconvertibility swaps might be better suited for corporate, private equity and other investors with money invested onshore because inconvertibility events often precede sovereign defaults. For example, Venezuela imposed convertibility restrictions in 2003 but has not defaulted on its debt. Notwithstanding, Exhibit 3 highlights that inconvertibility protection may sometimes trade cheaper than default protection. 

See additional important disclosures at the end of this report.

## **Morgan Stanley Quotient Analytics** 

## Emerging Markets Analytics 

**In this Primer,** we introduce the Morgan Stanley Quotient Analytics (MSQ) methodology and show how it can help users to analyze Emerging Market assets and potential trade ideas. In conjuction with the Analytics, the Plotting tool allows one to customize, monitor and share the analysis of trading opportunities. 

**Emerging Market Assets:** We have comprehensive coverage of Emerging Market assets, including currencies, implied volatilities, rates, sovereign credit and corporates. Screeners and Grids provide tools to analyze historical relationships and value within each of these assets and across countries and regions.

**Quotient Analytics** is a web-based application and provides a broad overview of the EM markets through markets’ Landing Pages, Analytic Screeners and Grids as well as Plotting Tool functionality. The markets pages highlight historical data and risk assets for relevant markets. The Screener is a powerful tool to filter and sort to identify market dislocations. The grid provides an overview of the asset surface through various standardized metrics used throughout the Morgan Stanley Quotient Analytics platform.

## **Quotient Analytics Navigation** 

This publication is an introduction to the Morgan Stanley Quotient Analytics platform, methodology and a quick reference for Emerging Markets investors who use relative value and cross-asset class analysis to identify trade opportunities. The interactive platform offers investors the opportunity to evaluate trades in terms of current levels, historical relationships and, in some cases, the cost of carry. We present example analysis along with detailed information on the metrics used. 

Quotient Analytics offers comprehensive coverage of Emerging Markets and breaks down the assets into three main classes: currencies, rates and credit. From here, we can find data about implied volatilities, sovereign credit and corporates, central bank policies and implement Charts, 

Screeners and Grids to efficiently scan and organize market data and historical relationships. 

The Emerging Markets section or the MSQ portal may be accessed by clicking at the appropriate tab on the main and blue menu at the upper end of the webpage. A gray submenu appears below the main menu with the subcategories: EM Currencies, EM Rates, etc. Some of the submenus contain lists of possible selections including Markets, Screeners and Grids. Published research and a list of contacts may also be obtained here. Please see Exhibit 1 for some details on the navigation of this EM overview page. Users are encouraged to explore all levels submenus to get a full perspective on potentialities of MSQ.

## **Best Carry** 

The third and last component of the Analytics Methodology is to compare the cost of carry as seen in Exhibit 9.  In addition to evaluating carry relative to other instruments, we recommend reviewing the historical level metric and carry metric in conjunction with each other. While an instrument may appear to be a good opportunity on a historical basis, the cost of carry may be significantly less for a similar security. The Screener tools are the best way to select and sort to indentify these relationships.

## **Filter Instrument Tenor** 

This result set shows the level differences and the inversion of some curves. As we apply the methodology described above, we assess how the levels compare with historical prices for these curves and how they compare with each other. 

Another approach to finding trades may be to compare the rolldown and carry of the assets. Rolldown represents the change in value of a swap as it rolls down the yield curve. For example, one year after entering a 10-year swap, the asset has rolled down to become a 9-year swap. The 1y rolldown, in basis points running, on a 10y swap is: 10y rate – 9y rate. Carry is the value of holding a swap to horizon; it is the difference between the coupon paid (fixed rate) and the floating rate. In basis points running, 1y carry on a 10y swap is equal to: 1y forward 9y rate – 10y rate). We can evaluate these independently or together, which encompasses the entire PnL in an unchanged environment.  Carry type filter selections are displayed in Exhibit 12. 

Exhibit 12

The level is low where the level is belly – 0.5 wing1 – 0.5 wing2. This means that the 5y rate is low compared to these other two from a historical perspective. If one wanted to play for a re-normalization, then one would sell 5s on the fly. This means that if the 5y rate increases relative to the others, the trade that would perform would be a short on the 5s and long on the wings.
